### 30 October 2026 telephone regression and merge record

- [ ] Latest caller-provided telephone transcript remains a failed acceptance run. The agent switched from Hindi to English without a clear language request, misclassified the caller's name as “Others,” failed to retain the caller name after it was repeated, requested the phone number again after the caller had already moved to date/time, and repeated the last-four phone confirmation after the caller said “हाँ ये सही है.” This is a shared conversation-state/language-routing failure and must be fixed across all agents, not only Workflow 22.
- [ ] Acceptance sequence: start in Hindi; request an appointment; provide a name; provide `9538300941` in spoken digit chunks; confirm with “हाँ ये सही है”; provide “कल” and then “5 PM” in separate turns. Require stable caller-selected Hindi until the caller switches language, retained name/number/date, one phone readback and confirmation, correct transition to time collection, no stale phone prompt, and no fabricated booking confirmation.
- [ ] Track each boundary independently: raw and selected STT language/text, persisted caller slots, booking slot/state transitions, LLM first useful text, TTS selected voice/language, first PCM and completed playback. A transcript or LLM-first-token value alone does not prove caller-heard speech.

### Latest verified voice-flow update — 29 October 2026

- A focused local TTS streaming trial reduces the later-phrase release threshold from 64 to 48 characters. The aggregator retains the full text and waits for a safe word boundary; its existing guard keeps recognized number/date sequences together for the covered languages. The focused suite `api/tests/test_natural_local_tts_phrases.py` passed **5 tests** on this worktree. This may reduce the wait for later audio phrases, but it increases synthesis request count by roughly one third and is **not yet verified in a live WebRTC or telephone call**. Keep it as a local candidate until actual audio-gap timings and quality are checked.
- The mixed spoken/numeric phone example `nine five 383 double zero 941` is already parsed as `9538300941` and read back one digit at a time by the current parser; its focused regression test passed. The observed live failures contain corrupted ASR words (for example, `nine-payer`), not clean input that the parser fails to handle. The self-hosted STT auto path selects one dominant-language decoder per utterance and does not decode Hindi-English code-switching inside that utterance. Do not force English for all phone capture: Hindi-spoken digits must remain valid. A temporary second English decode while a phone field is active is only a candidate; it needs real mixed-language audio and a VPS CPU/latency benchmark before implementation.
- The live Qwen `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M` runner was confirmed resident on the 4-core CPU VPS with its existing 4096 context. An idle snapshot showed low API/STT/TTS CPU, so it does not explain past call spikes. No model, service or live container was changed during this investigation; the current 64-to-48 TTS trial remains local and unshipped.
- Re-read the redacted stage events and aggregate metrics for completed Workflow 22 run **449** directly from the production database (no transcript or phone-number text was queried). Nine STT measurements ranged **0.563–1.429 s**; eight warm Speaches TTS first-byte measurements ranged **0.181–0.291 s**. Two LLM first-answer TTFB events were **10.348 s** and **15.339 s**. On the corresponding turns, the persisted `llm_first_text`→`tts_first_audio` stage gap was **1.885 s** and **1.875 s**. This confirms separate slow points: rare severe LLM waits and a pre-audio wait after first text; it does not establish the cause of the LLM outliers or prove caller-heard continuity. The local later-phrase trial cannot fix those LLM waits, and is not deployed.
- A single warm, synthetic Hindi request through the production API container to the same Ollama OpenAI-compatible endpoint returned its first non-empty text in **957 ms** for a **59-token prompt** (24-token response cap). Qwen remained resident; production has `OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=1`, `OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1`, and a 3-CPU quota. This verifies the normal warm path responds in about one second, not the full flow and not a p50/p95 benchmark. No other non-text Kodewaves workflow run overlapped the recorded run-449 window. The host's current load was **1.07/2.10/2.03** across 1/5/15-minute windows, but historical CPU telemetry for the 10–15 s turns is unavailable; short host-level load and the synthetic probe do not explain those outliers. The Ollama container sees the host root cgroup (`/proc/self/cgroup` is `0::/`), so its `cpu.stat` counters must not be represented as container-only throttling evidence.

## Current execution plan and limits — 27 October 2026

This section takes precedence over older experiment ordering below. It records the user's supplied acceleration plan and specific constraints. The user clarified that the plan is guidance to assess and execute, not an instruction to stop implementation. Historical evidence is retained with its original date and limits.

### Which supplied plan governs this work

`MASTER_VOICE_PIPELINE_PLAN.md` is the execution source of truth because it carries the full platform scope, the user's 700–800 ms aspiration and current 1,000–1,500 ms qualification target, CPU/server restrictions, language coverage, rollback rules, and the existing evidence across STT, LLM, TTS, WebRTC and telephony. The attached `Self-Hosted STT, TTS and LLM Latency Audit & 1.5-Second Plan` is a supporting audit, not a competing execution plan. Its measurement boundary (speech end to first non-silent client/carrier playback), correlated stage timings, p50/p95 reporting, full-workflow prompts, and combined CPU/concurrency profiling are adopted here.

The attached scan identifies itself as read-only work on the `stabilize` checkout. Its claim that auto STT is unavailable and its claim that the local TTS aggregator lacks partial phrase release are stale for this `codex/production-integration` checkout: the source configures detector/decoder support across the scheduled Indian languages plus English, and `speaches_tts.py` configures first-clause/first-phrase/later-phrase release in `LocalSpeechAggregator`. The live production STT manifest currently reports 15 installed languages with auto-detection enabled: Assamese, Bengali, Bodo, Dogri, Indian English, Hindi, Kannada, Maithili, Malayalam, Marathi, Nepali, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Tamil and Telugu. The production TTS manifests advertise voices for the same 15 languages; Hindi/Indian English use the bilingual Inaya voice and the other languages use their listed regional voices. This is the user's current requested scope after excluding Gujarati, Kashmiri, Konkani, Manipuri, Odia, Santali, Sindhi and Urdu. A production UI correction now resolves missing/stale local STT model IDs against the live inventory, so auto-detect and the installed-language choices remain visible and saveable. The live inventory and UI deployment are verified, but transcript/audio quality, switching within one utterance, and full WebRTC/telephone behavior still require acceptance calls. Keep provider/model-specific inventories, and do not infer accuracy or human-like quality from a capability list.

### Live CPU LLM check and warm-start correction — 27 October 2026

- Production Ollama is configured with only `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M` selected, a 4 GB container limit, three CPU cores and a 4096-token context. The resident model is about 1.9 GB on disk / 2.1 GB RSS in the current process. No model-selection change was made.
- A temporary `qwen3.5:0.8b` Ollama candidate was pulled and tested against four short Hindi, Indian-English, Hinglish and spoken-number prompts, then removed. It used about 1.32 GB on disk, generated only 2.6–3.9 tokens/sec in the tested prompts, and produced unreliable Hinglish and number replies. It is rejected and is no longer installed.
- The current 2B model's repeated warm 101-token Hindi prompt measured 1,137 ms prompt evaluation and 986 ms for 20 output tokens (20.29 tokens/sec; 2,173 ms total). This is a short direct Ollama probe, not the platform's full voice-turn latency. An earlier first inference on the same small prompt took 19,387 ms in prompt evaluation; after model activation, the repeated prompt was about 1.1 seconds. The first-use outlier points to an inference warm-start cost, but the direct probe alone does not establish the full production call's root cause.
- `scripts/warm_local_ollama.py` previously sent an empty prompt, which could load weights without exercising tokenization and inference. It now sends a fixed non-sensitive Hindi warmup with a two-token generation cap and keeps the selected model resident. The production warmup sidecar had been stopped (exit 137, not marked OOM, restart count 0); it is now running at 22.7 MiB of its 64 MiB limit. The configured 2B model is confirmed resident. Existing model selection and application API/UI images were left unchanged.
- Local compile and a mocked payload check passed. Server source hash matches the updated worktree file; a pre-change copy is kept at `/root/kodewaves-rollbacks/20261027-ollama-warmup/warm_local_ollama.py.before`. The sidecar's active reload path and a real call after it are not yet caller-verified. The 0.8B comparison and warmup probe did not establish a 700–800 ms or 1.0–1.5 s end-to-end result.

### Production ordinary-turn endpointing correction — 27 October 2026

- The live API source used a fixed 550 ms Silero VAD stop for every turn when local segmented STT was selected, even though phone-number collection was the only case that needed a longer pause. This added 350 ms to ordinary turns before STT could start.
- The API now defaults local-STT turns to 200 ms and switches to 550 ms only while the contact state says the caller is dictating a phone number. Explicit `vad_stop_secs` workflow overrides still take precedence. The existing contact-aware stop strategy keeps the 1.2 s number pause and returns to its 0.6 s normal pause.
- Focused worktree verification: `test_contact_turn_stop.py` and `test_run_pipeline_realtime_turn_config.py` passed **24 tests**. Production source compiled; the updated API image compiled the same files. Only the API container was recreated after confirming there were no active workflow runs. Live API health returned HTTP 200 with zero restarts, and the old image is tagged `kodewaves-api:rollback-vad-before-20261027` (previous image digest `sha256:4dcf2269290fb47028e4d06aabfd4948c865dd0481d0e1ef94e462466ebfd343`).
- This removes up to 350 ms of avoidable ordinary-turn endpointing delay on the local segmented-STT path. A fresh browser and telephone call is still needed to measure the full speech-end-to-audible result and check that normal speech is not fragmented; the overall latency and TTS continuity gates remain open.

### Production STT auto-detect selector and cache follow-up — 27 October 2026

- Root cause: the model-configuration editor looked up the selected STT model as the saved ID or the synthetic cloud value `auto`. The self-hosted catalog instead returns the concrete installed ID (`indicconformer-ctc-int8`); when the saved value was absent or stale, the editor had no selected model object, hid `multi`, showed a false “auto detection unavailable” warning, and could leave the select blank. The production STT sidecar itself was healthy and reported `supports_auto_detect: true`.
- The UI now resolves saved IDs against the live engine inventory, defaults local mode to its installed recognizer, and repairs missing/stale local IDs and language values before saving. Cloud `auto` behavior is preserved. Focused selector suite: **13 passed** with one thread. The default fork pool timed out on this Windows workstation before running tests; no speech-model inference was run locally.
- Production UI source backup: `/root/kodewaves-rollbacks/20261027-stt-auto-ui`. Deployed source hashes: `AIModelConfigurationV2Editor.tsx` `9a9498baf84592d97007d1423f92b75705bf8881623a9d8143e0f43c038ba8ca`; `sttEngineType.ts` `952e2cf7043d9230de6b42a07b86de6cc56381e8c8af10b2eecfae663b91bc39`. New image: `sha256:fe49124c54249d53746109270f7e051e88163280931e857d957638274485af0f`; previous image remains tagged `kodewaves-ui:rollback-stt-auto-before-20261027` (`sha256:4066ba81ce0dd6c5a73eb72227d70f470955673d6c2b34905695c408a8a9894c`). Only `kodewaves_ui` was recreated. It is healthy and `http://127.0.0.1:3010/api/health` returns HTTP 200. The API, STT, Ollama and TTS containers were not restarted by this UI deployment.
- Live inventories confirm the 15 requested STT/TTS languages listed above. This proves installed capability metadata and selector code only; a fresh user WebRTC call is still needed to prove persisted settings and caller-heard language/voice behavior.
- After the UI image build, Docker Buildx prune reclaimed **3.68 GB**. `docker system df` now reports **18.63 GB** Build Cache with only **43.11 MB** reclaimable; most of the accounting is shared with image layers, so further cache pruning would not materially free storage without removing image layers. Current root filesystem is **103 GB used / 90 GB free**; host RAM is **10 GB used / 5.6 GB available**. Model files, volumes, active and rollback images, and unrelated sites were preserved. Clearing build cache does not lower live inference-process RAM.

### Production regional TTS streaming warm-up correction — 27 October 2026

- Root cause: startup marked the regional TTS service ready after a normal synthesis warm-up, but live calls use the callback-driven streaming generator. The first streamed generation therefore remained cold. A Tamil request took **5.149 s** to first PCM before correction even though the service had passed health checks.
- `api/local_models/indic_vits/app.py` now warms the callback/streaming path itself for Tamil when Indus owns Hindi/Indian English, and for Hindi/English where those local models are active. Only this TTS container was rebuilt/recreated, after confirming no active calls. Rollback image tag: `kodewaves-indic-vits-tts:rollback-stream-warmup-20261027` (`sha256:2febbf89585b762e2841fb4ec8df167a8ccb9a7c3caf644449b6f54987756a03`). Deployed image: `sha256:08b150951470abb6757176bbd07b7ccac17fd54cb0aed965818bdd43803495d7`; service returned healthy.
- Production synthetic stream verification after deployment: Tamil first PCM/non-silent PCM **893 ms**, compared with **5,149 ms** before the fix (about **83% lower**); synthesis completed at **894 ms** for 1.76 s of audio. A sequential smoke pass across all **15 advertised language routes plus Hinglish** returned non-empty PCM for every request. Hindi, Indian English and Hinglish first non-silent audio measured **136–151 ms** in that pass; regional routes measured **0.98–1.44 s**. This was direct service synthesis, not a caller-heard WebRTC or telephone run, and it does not establish voice quality or gap-free playback.
- Storage follow-up: the earlier UI build already reclaimed **3.68 GB** of Buildx cache. Subsequent Docker accounting showed **18.63 GB** total build cache but only **43.11 MB** privately reclaimable because nearly all remaining cache is shared with image layers. A safe prune reclaimed **0 B**; removing model/DB/storage volumes or rollback/active images would risk production state. At the snapshot, the host had **90 GB free disk** and **5.6 GB available RAM**. Process memory was concentrated in Ollama/Qwen (~2.1 GiB), API (~980 MiB), STT (~898 MiB), Indus TTS (~1.28 GiB), and the regional VITS service (~355 MiB). This cache cleanup cannot solve inference RAM usage.
- Acceptance still open: obtain a fresh post-deploy browser call and authorized telephone call, correlate STT final → first useful LLM text → first non-silent TTS → actual playback, and inspect phrase gaps/cancellation. The route smoke test confirms service availability only; it does not certify conversational language quality or end-to-end latency.

### Production phone-number fragment correction — 27 October 2026

- A server-side reproduction showed that if STT delivered the word `double` and the following digit in separate turns, the contact parser lost the repeat marker and collected only one digit. It now retains a pending repeat marker for the next single digit, clears it on an unrelated utterance or explicit restart, and refuses to duplicate a joined two-digit alias. This does not repair an incorrect STT transcript or verify the caller's number.
- The deployed production file also lacked the existing worktree's canonical Hindi normalization and observed joined-digit aliases. Those changes were included in the same one-file update. The previous production code failed the split-double case and the observed joined-four-one case; the staged code passed both.
- Fourteen focused phone-number test functions were executed with Python's standard library against the staged file and again inside the new API image: **14/14 passed**. These are isolated parser checks, not live audio, STT accuracy, or conversation acceptance. Neither the server nor the local test runtime had pytest installed; no production database was used.
- Only the API container was recreated after both the API and fleet counters returned zero active calls. New image `sha256:26b035f6a91d5f1a66a5da46297d505b887eea9dbb3e6d68e000ccd3af4d1267` was built from the previous running image with just the contact-state file replaced. The parser now checks the previous caller turn before applying a pending marker, preventing an unrelated intervening request from doubling a later digit. The prior live image reproduced this failure; **15/15** isolated parser functions passed against the corrected source and inside the candidate image. These samples measure service timing, not detection accuracy, Hinglish fidelity, or regional-language quality.

### Production TTS number and date phrase correction — 27 October 2026

- The live phrase aggregator split a synthetic spoken phone number (`nine five three` / `eight three ...`) and date (`October twenty` / `seven ...`) into separate synthesis requests. Such splits can add a pause inside the information a caller needs to hear. The aggregator now waits across recognized English/Hindi number and date words and Unicode digits, including comma separators and a numeric `and`/`और` connector, before releasing a phrase. It preserves ordinary phrase streaming and resets pending content on interruption. Numeric words in other regional languages are not yet covered by this vocabulary and require their own acceptance work.
- Four focused asynchronous checks passed in the staged code and the candidate API image: English/Hindi number words, numeric dates and comma-separated digits; a character-by-character later phrase with a connector; interruption reset; and normal long-response streaming. These checks validate phrase output, not acoustic continuity. Holding a long number sequence may delay the first audio; measure that tradeoff in a real call.
- With both active-call counters at zero, only the API container was recreated. Live image `sha256:b2ed2c7d7be1bb791829cb5d1ac081c61232241435656b02f46ae030dc93e507` is healthy with zero restarts and HTTP health 200; its aggregator hash is `9e68c9e18902ffc0ca2d9e05f44b1bb31c128c57079cf9a0fccaa0912326e226`. A live-container synthetic check preserved the full sample number in one phrase. Rollback image: `kodewaves-api:rollback-tts-numbers-20261027`; source backup: `/root/kodewaves-rollbacks/20261027-tts-numbers/local_speech_aggregator.py.before`. WebRTC and telephone audio remain unverified.
- A subsequent server-side check ran 160 simulated chunk layouts across the English/Hindi number and date examples without losing text or splitting the tested sequences. Browser UI verification could not be performed because in-app browser use is disabled in the current Work settings.

### Production regional digit handling — 27 October 2026

- Before this update the live parser retained Bengali-script numerals as non-ASCII phone digits, and standalone Tamil and Kannada digit words were not captured. Numeric Unicode characters are now converted to ASCII digits, and a shared, bounded vocabulary handles standalone Bengali, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Punjabi and Marathi digit words in addition to the existing Hindi and English forms. The TTS phrase splitter uses the same words to keep recognized digit sequences together. The vocabulary uses Unicode CLDR number spellout data: https://github.com/unicode-org/cldr/tree/main/common/rbnf.
- **21 focused parser and aggregator checks passed** inside an isolated candidate API image, including previous split-double and interruption regressions, seven regional spoken-digit examples, non-ASCII numeric digits, and Tamil/Bengali TTS phrase grouping. A live-container check after deployment passed Bengali numeral normalization, Tamil and Kannada digit capture, and Tamil phrase grouping. These are synthetic text checks; they do not establish STT accuracy, date grammar, natural TTS, or coverage for every word variant and regional language.
- The API and fleet counters showed zero active calls before only the API container was recreated. The running image is `sha256:27f340bb2a0d6e162f7aa73c488088b4cf3171601aeecd908447883777658712`, healthy with zero restarts and HTTP health 200. Rollback image: `kodewaves-api:rollback-regional-digits-20261027`; prior source files: `/root/kodewaves-rollbacks/20261027-regional-digits`. A real browser and authorized telephone call remain required.

### Server voice timing and prompt-cache investigation — 27 October 2026

- With zero active calls, three sequential synthetic Hindi/Hinglish TTS requests produced first non-silent PCM in **157, 190 and 163 ms**, and 2.4–3.02 seconds of audio in 0.75–0.98 seconds per request. A simple 400 ms prebuffer playback simulation reported no underrun. It does not include LLM phrase arrival, Pipecat scheduling, network, browser or carrier playout and cannot establish uninterrupted live speech.
- A two-turn synthetic English Ollama request used a 149-token first prompt and the deployed 2B model with thinking disabled. First visible text took **1,685 ms** on the first turn, **773 ms** on the second; the follow-up reported **145 cached prompt tokens** out of 206. This is a short direct-model probe, not a real workflow or speech-end-to-audio benchmark. An active-call monitor was used; no conversation content or audio was saved.
- A separate in-container request-builder reproduction showed a cache limitation: when validated contact details exist, their reminder moves immediately before the latest user message, so a follow-up with the same details no longer has the prior request as an exact message prefix. The earlier request and follow-up shared only the first two leading messages. This demonstrates a prefix change, not its measured effect on a real call. A safe change must retain corrected contact facts and booking context, preserve history semantics, and be benchmarked before deployment; no prompt behavior was changed here.
- Evaluated an isolated candidate that retained prior reminders at their original turns and added corrected facts on later turns. Seven request-builder checks passed, but a small live-model comparison did **not** show the expected cache benefit: both old and candidate follow-ups reported **0 cached tokens**; first visible text was **3,498 ms** versus **3,292 ms**, respectively (one synthetic pair each, model outputs differed). This is insufficient to establish a useful improvement or quality parity. The candidate was discarded, production prompt behavior remains unchanged, and further investigation must verify token-level prompt rendering and reuse rather than assuming identical message prefixes guarantee a cache hit.
- Further controlled server checks with no active calls show prompt caching itself works with the current Qwen 3.5 renderer: an identical repeat reused **46/50** prompt tokens (105 ms first text), and a following user turn reused **46/87** (556 ms). A second synthetic comparison reused **37/78** tokens with one system message and **54/95** with two, so merely having two system messages does not explain the previous zero-cache result. Two deliberately length-limited first answers also reused **34/54** and **35/84** follow-up tokens, so truncation alone was not sufficient to reproduce it. Samples were small and synthetic; no root cause for the zero-cache workflow request is established. No server model settings or production code changed.

### Tool dispatch and stream error correction — 27 October 2026

- A controlled execution reproduced an error-path defect: after a tool call was marked dispatched, an LLM stream timeout still emitted a generic request to retry, potentially encouraging the caller to repeat an action already in progress. The recovery path now emits that retry only if neither spoken output nor a tool call was started. The error is still propagated; this change does not assert the tool succeeded or guarantee a spoken error after a dispatch.
- Five isolated candidate-image checks passed: empty completion, timeout before output, timeout after partial output, timeout after tool dispatch, and tool-only completion. Only the API was recreated after both active-call counters reported zero. The deployed image is `sha256:8819e971240ba775f952f3172b14764e4f4e9d26b3059f1ea5acc6ee50881038`; running source hash `d210940a36276208b2aa56d0ce1e3179e41aed7400aa70fe96e32480e871f8a1`, healthy with zero restarts and HTTP health 200. Rollback image: `kodewaves-api:rollback-tool-timeout-20261027`; source backup: `/root/kodewaves-rollbacks/20261027-tool-timeout/booking_tool_choice.py.before`. ### Isolated text pipeline checks — 27 October 2026

- The deployed API image ran a synthetic English-language-switch turn through the actual text pipeline and retained the English preference in its checkpoint. A second isolated run completed two turns: it captured an explicitly supplied name after a name/phone prompt and recalled the same name on the next turn. The model was replaced with a mock that fails on any unexpected LLM request; database/configuration/storage and workflow inputs were mocked, network was disabled, and no production run or external action was created.
- The first isolation attempt failed because the test fixture attempted to initialize MinIO despite network isolation; a recording-audio fetcher stub allowed the test to run. The isolated container still logged an unavailable optional MinIO integration, so its startup time is not a production latency measurement. ### Synthetic TTS interruption probe — 27 October 2026

- With active-call monitoring enabled, a synthetic Hindi gateway request was closed after its first audio, followed immediately by a second request. First audio arrived at **1,105 ms** and **1,518 ms**, respectively; the second completed with nonempty PCM. The Indus service reported **0 ms queue wait** for both and logged only two chunks for the interrupted request. This does not show client playback, prove stale audio cannot leak, or prove the first request was fully cancelled before the next started.
- Three subsequent sequential repeats of the second phrase reached first PCM at **220, 188 and 113 ms** through the gateway; each produced nonempty audio. This small comparison shows substantial variability around the interruption, but does not isolate whether concurrent cleanup, inference warm-up or CPU contention caused the slower sample. The warm samples do not establish end-to-end or telephone latency; no production model or configuration was changed.
- A subsequent monitored, synthetic comparison with no active calls ran two identical short requests sequentially and then concurrently. Sequential synthesis completed in **570/541 ms**, with first audio at **107/89 ms**; concurrent synthesis completed in **1,410/1,418 ms**, with first audio at **219/149 ms**. This indicates a throughput penalty when two TTS requests overlap on this VPS, but one sample pair does not establish the best worker setting or performance under real calls. The Indus container is limited to two CPUs with two model workers and one OpenMP/MKL thread each; Ollama is limited to three CPUs, while STT and the regional gateway are each limited to two CPUs. These limits can overlap on the four-core host.
- A separate single short LLM-plus-TTS comparison showed TTS baseline **113 ms first / 566 ms total**, overlapping **152 ms first / 662 ms total**; LLM first text was **565 ms baseline / 156 ms overlapping**, confounded by an identical-prompt cache hit. This small sample does not prove a general LLM/TTS contention effect. Keep the two workers until repeated real workload, interruption and concurrency checks can establish whether a different setting improves latency without harming users.

### Automatic recognition language-state correction — 27 October 2026

- A simulated STT response reproduced a state defect: punctuation-only text was filtered from the transcript, but its confident Tamil detection had already replaced a prior stable Hindi language. A later uncertain segment could then use the wrong fallback. The API adapter now updates stable auto-detection language only after nonempty, non-punctuation text has been accepted.
- Five isolated checks passed for empty text, punctuation-only text, confident Tamil, confident Hindi and the existing English rescue route. These are mocked recognizer responses; they do not verify detector accuracy or prove that this was the cause of a particular live call's wrong script. Before API recreation both active-call counters were zero. Deployed image `sha256:e5e448122947c30b0df5a8eb64be950cbea78fa6c1a3db4cff0d546af7d0a30a` is healthy with zero restarts and HTTP health 200; source hash `c70498047c5fe592ea3af8968fac239a1f7a20be23b7bc0155fcf146651fd219`. Rollback image: `kodewaves-api:rollback-stt-stability-20261027`; source backup: `/root/kodewaves-rollbacks/20261027-stt-stability/speaches_stt.py.before`. Human-audio language detection, mixed speech and real-call playback remain open.
- Six in-memory synthetic TTS → automatic STT samples selected their intended languages (Bengali, Kannada, Malayalam, Marathi, Tamil and Telugu) and returned nonempty text. This verifies route selection on one generated phrase per language, not transcript accuracy, human voices, code-switching or regional coverage generally. End-to-end STT service times were **15,781 / 3,981 / 3,808 / 4,838 / 1,196 / 1,055 ms** respectively. The first Bengali trace spent **13,980 ms in language detection**; the Kannada/Malayalam/Marathi traces spent **3,609 / 3,487 / 4,482 ms in decoder work**, which includes potential model loading. The STT service had already completed its startup detector warm-up and had zero restarts; the exact reason for the first detection spike remains unproven.
- After the other languages, two more Bengali synthetic requests took **1,036 ms** and **621 ms**; the traces recorded detection **316/419 ms** and decoder **710/196 ms**. This is evidence of costly variable cold or residency effects, not proof that all repeat calls will be fast. Peak observed STT container memory after these samples was about **1.21 GiB of 2 GiB**. Calls were monitored and each sample remained in memory; no audio or transcript was stored. Do not preload all recognizers or weaken language detection without a measured memory and accuracy comparison.
- Production STT currently has **three resident slots**: Hindi and English are protected and the third rotates among regional languages. In an isolated, network-disabled container with the production model volume mounted read-only, a four-slot probe loaded both detectors and Hindi, English, Bengali and Kannada. Its peak process RSS was **1,338 MiB** under an **1,800 MiB** test limit. A second Bengali model lookup was **0 ms** after Kannada was loaded, versus **800 ms** on initial Bengali load in that same probe. A separate detector-free probe peaked at **870 MiB**. This supports evaluating four slots to reduce regional reloads, but both probes used one second of silence, did not exercise real voice lengths, service memory overhead, concurrent calls or accuracy, and cannot establish safe headroom under the production **2 GiB** limit. Production remains at three slots; model files and services were unchanged.

Likewise, phrase aggregation code and its focused local regression do not prove uninterrupted caller-heard speech. The unresolved work is a fresh production trace that joins speech end, STT final, first useful LLM text, phrase/TTS queue and first non-silent audio at the WebRTC/telephone output boundary. Historical high Qwen delays and telephone failures remain evidence of the old measured runs only; they are not a new post-change baseline. Production is not considered updated unless runtime revision and a fresh call confirm it.

### Current requested language coverage and remaining all-22 expansion — 27 October 2026

The source recognizer defines decoder/detector labels for all 22 scheduled Indian languages plus Indian English, but the live production `/v1/models` inventory is the authority for downloaded decoders. It reports 15 installed languages with auto-detection enabled: Assamese, Bengali, Bodo, Dogri, English (India), Hindi, Kannada, Maithili, Malayalam, Marathi, Nepali, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Tamil and Telugu. The TTS manifests now report installed voices for the same 15. These are the 14 scheduled Indian languages plus English the user currently wants. **Gujarati, Kashmiri, Konkani, Manipuri, Odia, Santali, Sindhi and Urdu remain excluded by the user's explicit scope**; they are not being counted as unfinished requirements for this scope. A request to expand back to all 22 would require downloading and validating the additional eight STT decoders and finding matching TTS routes.

The matching capability lists do not prove recognition accuracy, code-switch fidelity, TTS pronunciation, perceived accent, gender consistency, or full-call playback. Auto language selection is per STT request/utterance; switching languages inside one utterance is not established. Continue to report model/provider-specific capabilities and never infer a shared-language route merely from a broad model card.

Source for the existing sherpa-onnx repository's language files, code-switch variants, format, and sizes: https://huggingface.co/parismitaglobalsolutions/indicconformer-sherpa-onnx/blob/main/README.md

**Auto-detection misroute correction:** the resolver returns an explicit unsupported-language HTTP error for a confident result rather than silently relabeling it as Hindi; low-confidence results may retain the prior supported language or configured fallback. Isolated resolver checks passed for confident-unsupported rejection, low-confidence prior-language retention and supported English. The current production resolver source matches the worktree, and its API image is deployed; those particular unsupported-language branches have not been exercised in a production call. The production STT sidecar advertises the 15 currently installed routes above; this does not make the other eight excluded routes installed.

Research found two relevant candidates, neither accepted as a replacement yet:

- **AI4Bharat IndicConformer multilingual 600M checkpoint** is an official candidate for closing the STT coverage gap: its upstream repository lists a single `multi` checkpoint and monolingual releases for all 22 scheduled languages. The upstream setup uses AI4Bharat NeMo; this does not establish compatibility with the current local CTC service, streaming behavior, code-switch accuracy, model memory, or 4-core CPU latency. Benchmark the multilingual artifact on the VPS before changing the selector or replacing the current Hindi/English routes. Source: https://github.com/AI4Bharat/IndicConformerASR
- **AI4Bharat Indic Parler-TTS (0.9B)** is a possible broad-coverage TTS candidate: the model card states 20 scheduled Indian languages plus English, with recommended speakers, and Apache-2.0 licensing. It does not list Kashmiri or Punjabi as official support, does not establish Hinglish/code-switch continuity, and does not provide a 4-core CPU streaming latency result. Its larger autoregressive model makes it a quality/coverage bake-off candidate, not an assumed low-latency replacement. Source: https://huggingface.co/ai4bharat/indic-parler-tts

**Current adapter mismatch:** `api/local_models/indic_stt/app.py` serves sherpa-onnx `OfflineRecognizer.from_nemo_ctc` over per-language INT8 ONNX files; the official IndicConformer 600M multilingual release is distributed and documented through the separate AI4Bharat NeMo stack. It is therefore not a checkpoint-only replacement for this service. Evaluate it as a separate adapter/runtime candidate, and require a measured parity path before adding it to selectable models. Source detector/decoder constants describe more languages than the live installation necessarily contains; `/v1/models` is authoritative for installed language files and auto-detection readiness. The matching TTS `/v1/models` inventory is authoritative for voices actually installed. Expanding either configured set without matching weights/voices and a successful decode/synthesis test would over-advertise capability.

- **AI4Bharat Indic-TTS** documents 15 languages including Indian English, Gujarati, Manipuri, and Odia. It could close three of the present local gaps, but not Kashmiri, Konkani, Santali or Sindhi; the referenced implementation uses per-language checkpoints and PyTorch/Coqui setup. No same-host CPU latency/quality benchmark or streaming adapter is established. Source: https://github.com/AI4Bharat/Indic-TTS/tree/master/inference
- **Bodhan AI / AI4Bharat Indic-Speak** advertises 22 Indian languages plus English and one continuous multilingual voice, with 13 production languages and 10 preview languages. The model card specifies a 3.78B-parameter BF16 Llama-3.2 speech LM plus a Vocos decoder, requires gated access, reports an approximately 200 ms response figure without a matching 4-core VPS benchmark, and warns style control is preview quality. Its license permits self-hosting its own product, but requires prior written approval for API hosting for other parties absent a stated exception and prohibits robocalls/auto-dialers. Kodewaves has campaign/telephony features, so this license and use-case compatibility must be resolved before even evaluating it for production. It is not currently an approved universal replacement. Sources: https://huggingface.co/bodhan-ai/indic-speak and https://github.com/Bodhan-AI/bodhan-model-info/blob/main/licenses/indic-open-model-license/v1/Indic_Open_Model_License.md
- **HyperneuronAI Quipus 0.6B Speech v2** now claims 22 scheduled Indian languages under MIT, but its own card says English is future roadmap work, formal latency/quality metrics are absent, samples for the 18 newly added languages are not uploaded, and its ~120 ms TTFB / 2× real-time claim is inherited from the old four-language checkpoint, not remeasured on v2. Streaming inference is listed as future work, while the provided inference uses vLLM and CUDA. It is a plausible regional-language research candidate, not a verified CPU/streaming replacement or a complete Hindi+Indian-English+Hinglish solution. The repo is gated; access terms were not accepted and weights were not downloaded. Source: https://huggingface.co/hyperneuronAILabs/quipus-0.6-speechv2

The next language-coverage decision must test eligible replacements for each missing language on the target CPU, with explicit voice/grammar quality and license/use-case gates. Do not expand `AUTO_DETECT_LANGUAGES` by itself: that would turn unsupported languages into silent wrong-language TTS or fallback replies.

### Required outcome and measurement boundary

Measure `actual speech end → STT final → first useful LLM text → first non-silent TTS PCM → client playback`. The current practical qualification target is 1,300–1,500 ms for warm Hindi, Indian English and Hinglish; the original 700–800 ms objective remains visible and unachieved. Stage budgets are hypotheses, not current performance claims:

| Stage | Proposed warm budget | Proof required |
|---|---:|---|
| VAD / endpointing | ≤200 ms | Actual last speech sample to confirmed endpoint; no fragmented names/numbers |
| STT final | ≤250 ms | Request start to accurate final mixed-language transcript |
| LLM first useful text | ≤350 ms | Request start to usable response text, excluding reasoning/empty chunks |
| TTS first non-silent PCM | ≤250 ms | Request start to speech-energy audio, with queue wait reported separately |
| Transport / playback | ≤250 ms | Server speech PCM to measured client/browser or carrier reception/playback |
| Complete turn | ≤1,300–1,500 ms | Direct speech-end-to-audible measurement; do not add overlapping durations twice |

The release report must state whether each target is evaluated at p50 or p95, include slow/failed turns, and give sample counts. A median pass cannot support a claim that p95 passed. Browser/carrier receipt is not automatically proof of physical sound at the listener's ear; label the actual measurement boundary.

### Specific restrictions and tuning caps

- Execute future inference and performance tests on the four-core, 16 GB CPU-only VPS. The user's later server-only instruction supersedes historical local-PC experiments. Preserve unrelated sites and all existing source changes.
- Keep the currently selected models/services until a replacement passes the identical corpus, correctness, audio, resource, rollback and transport gates. Model count or download size alone is not an acceptance criterion.
- The user clarified that languages named in the supplied plan are examples/priorities, not a language cap. Hindi, Indian English and Hinglish are the first qualification group; the other supported languages remain in scope and must be qualified individually. Do not hide or disable them just because the optimization plan names only three cases. Earlier explicit language exclusions remain recorded separately; preserve the original full-language requirement as unresolved rather than claiming it complete.
- A 48–80-token answer is a proposed conversational budget. It must not truncate speech, dates, number readbacks, required explanations or tool arguments. Detect length-limit completions and finish or recover explicitly; tool-call budgets require separate validation.
- A 12–24-character first clause is a proposed test range, not permission to split arbitrary text. Never break words, phone numbers, dates, currency or abbreviations. Compare first audible latency and subsequent gaps together.
- Endpointing trials span 150–500 ms. Select the shortest setting that preserves complete utterances; use longer dictation pauses only while collecting numbers/dates, with a bounded return to normal endpointing.
- Budget inference threads against the actual four cores across overlapping stages. Container CPU limits, model thread counts and simultaneous workers are different controls; thread-count sums alone do not prove absence of contention.
- Concurrency qualification is one and two calls. Define bounded queue capacity, maximum queue wait, cancellation and overload behavior before increasing worker count.
- No idle synthetic generation loops. Resident model memory is acceptable only within the documented budget; measure idle CPU separately from active-call usage.
- Preserve tenant isolation, action consent, known caller context, configuration overrides and rollback. Do not commit or push tests, diagnostic scripts, call data or reports to GitHub.

### One ordered path to implementation and acceptance

| Step | Planned work | Exit gate / dependency | Current status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Freeze baseline and traces | Reconcile current revisions, images, model IDs, voice routes and resource settings. Correlate speech end, VAD stop, STT request/final, LLM request/first useful token, speakable text, TTS request/queue/PCM/non-silent PCM, playback, interruption and cancellation under one turn ID. | Trace completeness audit and one reproducible complete turn before optimization. | Existing stage/phrase/output traces cover parts of this; full correlation and playback proof remain open. |
| 2. Shared corpus | Include Hindi, Indian English, Roman/mixed-script Hinglish, names, number/date dictation, short/long replies, known-contact reuse, booking consent/refusal, language switch, unavailable lookup and interruption. | Baseline and candidates use identical inputs and settings; human speech and generated clips are labeled separately. | Partial synthetic/service probes exist; complete real-call corpus remains open. |
| 3. STT qualification | Keep explicit language as a faster option. Qualify a genuinely multilingual streaming recognizer for Hindi/English/Hinglish. Test LID plus candidate recognition in parallel only if accuracy, RAM and CPU results improve. Keep regional IndicConformer on its existing LRU/resident pool; do not replace it with cache clearing or preload all models blindly. | Accurate mixed-language transcript, stable numbers/names, measured endpointing and cold/warm cost; only installed and qualified capabilities advertised. | Existing IndicConformer + English Moonshine + LID are deployed. Within-utterance Hinglish and general auto-language quality remain unaccepted. |
| 4. LLM critical path | Keep one selected model resident using the serving API's supported keep-alive form. Audit token-budgeted history, stable system/tool prefixes, current-node tools, thinking controls and secondary inference. Preserve deterministic greetings, clarification, language-switch, unavailable-action and booking paths. | Quality and grounded tool use pass before choosing shorter prompts, smaller models or lower response budgets. No missing caller facts or false action claims. | Long uncached prompt processing and poor Hindi answers reproduced. Shorter generic guidance saved time but failed quality and was not deployed. |
| 5. TTS and output | Tune natural first clauses; prewarm selected voices only when justified. Preserve playback-timed audio/text ordering and final flush. Cache exact repeated phrases with organization/configuration/voice identity. Verify bounded workers, queues, cancellation and no stale audio after interruption. | First non-silent PCM and complete audible response pass together; no clicks, clipped endings, missing words, voice jumps or growing queue waits. | Two Indus workers already deployed. A paced output test had ≤4 ms delivery gaps; real WebRTC/telephone continuity remains open. |
| 6. Capability and settings | Maintain language → STT model → LLM language policy → TTS model → voice → true streaming granularity → quality status. Reconcile admin, organization and workflow selectors, save/reload and runtime provenance. | Unsupported choices cannot be selected; experimental auto-detection is not presented as fully qualified. Every advertised language has evidence. | Current paired inventory is 15 languages; manifests and synthetic audio alone do not prove human quality. |
| 7. Real transport qualification | Run warm/cold, concurrency one/two, short/long response cases on WebRTC and designated authorized telephone numbers. Collect p50/p95/max, errors, audio gaps, CPU and peak RAM. Do not benchmark during unrelated active calls. | At least 30 warm turns per primary language/mixed-language case and five cold runs, with matrix coverage and counts reported. Complete sustained ten-turn conversations and interruption tests. | Open. No full transport acceptance is established by the recent service/mock-output tests. |
| 8. Controlled deployment | Snapshot sources, image digests, model/config state and active branch. Drain calls; change affected services only. Align required local service contracts in both production Compose variants, and verify model-loaded readiness. | Same saved configuration works after deploy; rollback is exercised; remove replaced services only after acceptance. | Existing rollback snapshots exist; each future release requires a fresh gate. |
| 9. Remaining platform | Resume all user/admin journeys in section 6 after the core voice gate, carrying the same capability and caller-context rules through campaigns, CRM, appointments, tools, widgets, recordings, billing and reports. | Evidence per journey, organization isolation, failure/retry cases and live deployment provenance. | Open; voice progress does not establish platform completion. |

### Trace coverage audit — planning only, 27 October 2026

### First-phrase PCM buffer adjustment — source change, 27 October 2026

The active worktree's combined Rasa route buffered 400 ms of the first PCM before releasing it, while its TTS iterator requests approximately 200 ms chunks. This creates a known first-audio hold of up to one extra output chunk. The source setting is now 200 ms (one chunk) to reduce that intentional hold while retaining a small bridge for sequential phrase synthesis. This is a local source change only: it has not been deployed or measured in a live call, and reduced buffering could reintroduce phrase gaps. The next caller-heard comparison must measure speech-end-to-playback and inter-phrase gaps together before accepting the tradeoff.

Read-only inspection of the current worktree identifies the following implementation work before the proposed corpus can produce a trustworthy end-to-end report. This is a source audit, not a claim that every path ran in production.

| Measurement | Existing source / limitation | Planned gap to close |
|---|---|---|
| Speech end and VAD stop | Pipecat `user_bot_latency_observer.py` handles `VADUserStoppedSpeakingFrame`; estimates actual speech end as `frame.timestamp - frame.stop_secs`. | Export both the observed VAD timestamp and estimated speech end under the same turn ID; label this estimate and compare it to the known waveform boundary in a replay. |
| STT request / final | `speaches_stt.py::_transcribe` starts a monotonic clock. `realtime_feedback_observer.py` logs `stt_final`. Segment processing can combine queued fragments before finalizing. | Correlate queue entry, decoder/LID request, server response and final utterance emission; count fragment merges and distinguish transport time from recognition. |
| LLM request / first output | `booking_tool_choice.py::get_chat_completions` traces HTTP headers, first event, first output, prompt/cache/output token counts and completion reason. Its first-output field accepts either content or tool calls. | Add the common turn ID and distinguish first non-whitespace spoken content, tool-call output and first useful/speakable phrase. A tool-call delta must not be reported as audible answer readiness. |
| Speakable phrase / TTS request | `speaches_tts.py` aggregates clauses and traces each synthesis generator using a context ID. | Record phrase-ready and HTTP request timestamps separately, then map the phrase/context IDs to the caller turn. Preserve ordering through cache-hit and cache-miss paths. |
| TTS queue and synthesis | `indus_tts/app.py` logs queue wait, first PCM and synthesis totals. `AVAILABLE_MODELS` limits model instances, while requests can wait on `get()` without an explicit request admission timeout in this handler. | Correlate provider request IDs; define queue capacity, maximum wait and cancellation before changing concurrency. Bounded model workers do not imply a bounded pending-request queue. |
| First PCM / speech-energy PCM | Phrase traces measure first `TTSAudioRawFrame`; the output observer separately detects non-silent output using an RMS threshold. These are different pipeline boundaries. | Record first non-silent PCM at synthesis and output boundaries, with sample rate, threshold and audio offset, rather than treating HTTP bytes as audible speech. |
| Server output delay / gaps | `realtime_feedback_observer.py::_observe_voice_audio` starts its per-turn timer at `TranscriptionFrame`, then records output frames, non-silent frame time and internal silence. | Its current origin is STT final, not actual speech end. Join it to the VAD trace instead of labeling the current duration full response latency. |
| Existing response-delay metric | `run_pipeline.py` registers the user/bot latency observer; `useWebSocketRTC.tsx` displays received `rtf-latency-measured` events. | Preserve the existing metric, document its server-side measurement boundary, and avoid presenting receipt/display of that event as client playback evidence. |
| Browser / carrier playback | The inspected WebRTC UI consumes server latency events; no client playback acknowledgment was established by this audit. Recording-player `currentTime` code concerns saved audio, not live-call playout. | Add or reuse actual live peer/carrier receive/playout telemetry; identify clock synchronization and uncertainty. Keep WebRTC peer, browser speaker and carrier boundaries separate. |
| Cancellation / interruption | TTS `_handle_interruption` stops keepalive work and clears turn state. The feedback observer returns early for interruption/end/cancel frames after its earlier processing. | Record cancellation requested, upstream generation closed, provider worker released and last delivered old-turn audio; test ghost audio and resource release, not only receipt of the interruption frame. |

The next executable task, when implementation resumes, is therefore to unify these existing signals and fill their missing boundaries. Adding another independent duration counter or running the full corpus before correlation is complete would not satisfy Phase 1.

### Evidence that constrains the next implementation decision

On 26 October, a VPS test using the production TTS adapter and Pipecat paced mock output delivered 8.08 seconds of Hindi audio with a maximum delivery gap of 3 ms and 200 ms maximum internal silence. Adding the real Qwen stream produced first text in 4,453 ms and first output in 5,669 ms, with a maximum delivery gap of 4 ms; the Hindi reply was semantically poor. These tests isolate components and omit a real WebRTC peer, microphone, STT and telephone carrier; they do not close the live interruption issue.

Prompt-cache probes found an exact-repeat request with 179 cached prompt tokens at 106 ms first text, but a new question with the same system prefix had zero reported cached tokens and took about two seconds. A one-token prefix-prime request also failed to warm a different question (zero cached tokens; 1,880 ms first text), so background prefix priming is not an approved optimization. Three compact-guidance probes reduced first text from 2,074/1,878/1,961 ms to 1,230/1,146/1,419 ms, but produced poor Hindi answers; the compact guidance was not deployed. Changing sampling penalties also did not establish correctness. Optimize only after preserving reply quality.

The narrow Whisper English rescue was deployed separately on 26 October and passed two live service samples at 315–357 ms; Hindi/Tamil checks still chose their expected decoder. It misses some short English examples and is not a general multilingual or within-utterance code-switch recognizer. Keep this limitation in auto-detection acceptance.

## 1. Scope and non-negotiable requirements

Finish the existing platform, preserving unrelated sites, existing changes and tenant isolation. Prioritize CPU-only Hindi, Indian-accented English and Hindi/English code-switching with stable voice identity, grammatical replies, natural pacing and uninterrupted audio. Regional language coverage remains a separately tracked requirement; do not silently remove working regional voices to claim a single-engine success. Urdu is excluded from the user's requested active voice catalog.

The latest user-requested response target is 1,000–1,500 ms from speech end to the first audible answer; 700–800 ms remains the original aspirational target. Neither is an achieved measurement. Measure speech-end to first audible playback, not only model inference or the arrival of text. No fabricated readiness, fallback, billing, model capabilities or timing claims.

## 2. Verified starting state and corrections

Direct VPS inspection on 22 October found `kodewaves_indic_vits_tts` and `kodewaves_indus_tts` still healthy and running. Kokoro is a candidate, not the active replacement. `/root/tts_test` contains four WAVs, a 311 MB `kokoro-v1.0.onnx` and a 27 MB voices file. Disk weight size is not container image size, resident RAM or peak RAM. A 150 MB service and either 1.2 GB or 2.5 GB savings are not established.

The supplied benchmark reports full synthesis: Hindi female 2.30 s / 6.21 s audio, Hindi male 2.45 s / 7.27 s, Hinglish 3.49 s / 6.89 s, English 2.65 s / 4.76 s. WAV presence was verified; those timing values remain supplied evidence pending reproducible execution and retained raw output. RTF below one means faster than audio playback, not sub-second response. These totals do not prove first audio in 180–200 ms. The historical `run/deploy-kokoro-cpu.log` ends with a 14 October deployment using Piper/Whisper and is not evidence of current Kokoro integration.

Official Kokoro documentation lists Hindi voices `hf_alpha`, `hf_beta`, `hm_omega`, `hm_psi`; `af_heart` is American English, not an Indian English voice. Stock Kokoro is not a replacement for all scheduled Indian languages. The identity and revision of any separately named “Goonj” adaptation must be supplied by an actual repository/model artifact and verified; do not equate that name with the downloaded stock model.

Sources: https://huggingface.co/hexgrad/Kokoro-82M ; https://huggingface.co/hexgrad/Kokoro-82M/blob/main/VOICES.md ; https://github.com/thewh1teagle/kokoro-onnx

## 3. Execution order and release gates

1. Snapshot current local/server branch, exact dirty files, service configuration, image digests and selected runtime model IDs. Preserve changes in both checkouts. Keep local-only diagnostics and reports out of GitHub. Inspect ongoing processes before starting competing builds or benchmarks.
2. Complete the correlated baseline and shared corpus described above before qualifying any replacement. Reproduce a candidate benchmark only when evidence identifies the stage it can improve. Pin source revisions, dependency versions and artifact checksums; record CPU allocation, threads, RAM, queue load, cold load and warm inference. Do not replace the selected live engine during qualification.
3. Validate pronunciation and voice identity with native listening: Devanagari Hindi, Roman Hindi, English, mixed-script Hinglish, names, dates, currency, contact numbers, appointments and short acknowledgements. Test both requested genders. A non-silent WAV is not a naturalness or accent pass. Indian English must be demonstrated; `af_heart` cannot be relabeled Indian.
4. Implement the candidate service and adapters behind an explicit feature/configuration switch only after capability checks. Verify contract and actual audio before catalog promotion.
5. Run text chat, WebRTC and authorized telephony with the same saved configuration and contact context. Compare to baseline, including cold and warm turns, interruption and sustained conversations.
6. Cut over only after the replacement passes required language/voice and transport checks. Drain active calls, snapshot rollback state, deploy only affected application services, verify health and saved configurations. Remove obsolete running TTS containers after acceptance; retain rollback artifacts until the replacement is stable. Delete only identified unused model caches not referenced by active services or rollback requirements.

## 4. Speech implementation map

| Area | Required implementation | Acceptance evidence |
|---|---|---|
| STT | The 26 October paired inventory contains 15 languages, using IndicConformer, Moonshine English, Anuvani LID and an optional narrow Whisper English rescue. Older 23-label model inventory is not current active pipeline coverage. Detection is utterance-level, not within-utterance segmentation; realistic caller and mixed-language qualification remain open. | Per-language native corpus, Indian-accent English, names/numbers, code-switch turns, confidence calibration, silence, latency and save/reload |
| LLM | Keep the selected model until a candidate beats it on measured latency AND instruction/tool/grammar accuracy. Verify exact Qwen version, quantization and thinking controls; do not promise 250 ms from model size. Bound prompt/history and prevent unnecessary cache invalidation. | Cold/warm first token, first speakable phrase, complete response, tool correctness and empty-response rates |
| TTS service | Production routes Hindi and Indian English through the shared Indus Inaya voice behind the VITS gateway; regional VITS covers 15 listed languages total including Hindi/English. The Indus container is actively configured and must not be stopped as unused. The live VITS manifest does not cover 8 scheduled languages. Qualify a replacement for CPU, latency, licensing, code-switching, voice consistency and all required languages before changing the route. | WAV/PCM contracts, non-empty output, first audible chunk, full generation, voice continuity, language accuracy, memory, idle CPU and telephony playback |
| Resolver | Update `api/services/configuration/kodewaves_resolver.py` and the installed catalog from actual service capabilities. Resolve source/model/voice/language once per run, preserve overrides and reject unsupported combinations. | Round-trip configuration, stale voice migration, disabled/unavailable engine and tenant checks |
| Streaming | Update `api/services/pipecat/speaches_tts.py` and `run_pipeline.py` as needed. Preserve completion/error frames, ordered chunks, cancellation and final text flush. HTTP chunking of a fully synthesized WAV is not model streaming. | No missing/duplicated words, no truncated endings, no empty successful contexts, barge-in without ghost audio |
| Voice selection | Treat voice as timbre/gender preference; language routing follows supported output text/language policy. Keep stable identity across Hindi/English where demonstrated. | Same-speaker listening comparisons and mixed-language turns |
| UI/admin | Sync model and voice selectors, language capabilities, status and demos on organization and agent pages. Explain unsupported auto-detection clearly and never advertise unavailable voices. | Save/reload/edit/publish/demo/live-call agreement |
| Deployment | Compose, environment, health probes, resource budgets, logs and rollback must match the service actually selected. | Service/image/model IDs in run evidence and clean idle resource samples |

## 5. Timing and audio acceptance protocol

Record one correlated turn ID with wall-clock UTC timestamps and monotonic durations: speech end, VAD finalization, STT final text, LLM request, first token, first speakable text, TTS request, first PCM, first audible non-silent PCM and browser/carrier playback. Report queue time separately. Capture failures and timeouts rather than omitting slow turns.

Use at least 30 warm turns per main language/mixed-language case, plus at least five cold runs. Report p50, p95, maximum, error count, audio duration and resource contention for concurrency one and two. Compare baseline and candidate on the same texts/hardware/settings. Run at least one sustained ten-turn conversation per primary case to catch voice changes, pauses, duplicate replies and context growth. Do not label the 750–950 ms budget achieved without end-to-end evidence.

Investigate gaps by comparing text availability, synthesis queue/inference, PCM continuity and transport playback. Use sensible phrase boundaries; tiny fragments can worsen prosody and CPU overhead. Test punctuation, numbers and sentence endings. No full-stop clicks, abrupt final samples, silent completions, sentence restarts or unexpected speaker/accent changes in the acceptance corpus. User listening acceptance is recorded separately from automated waveform checks.

## 6. Complete platform work and evidence

This is an update to `PLATFORM_COMPLETION_PLAN.md`, not a declaration that its phases are complete. Maintain route/action coverage and record revision, environment, reproduction, result and remaining limits for every feature below.

| Domain | Completion requirements |
|---|---|
| Identity/workspaces | Signup/login/logout, sessions, workspace switching, admin/user permissions and cross-tenant negative tests |
| Provider/settings | Master keys/BYOK/local sources, model/voice synchronization, revocation, secrets masking and runtime provenance |
| Agents/workflows | Create/edit/publish/version, prompts, tools, saved voice/language, text chat, WebRTC and error recovery |
| Telephony | Carrier validation, signed callbacks/media access, inbound/outbound, codec conversion, transfer/hangup, capacity and reconciliation; only designated authorized test numbers |
| Campaigns/CRM | Exact CSV number preservation, contacts/names reused, scheduling/pause/retry, durable jobs and no duplicate dispatch |
| Contact-aware conversations | Reuse known data across all workflows, not only booking. Confirm the known number before using it when needed; request a replacement only after rejection or missing data. Never invent contact/order details. |
| Appointments | Availability, timezone, booking/reschedule/cancel, duplicate prevention, contact confirmation and persisted result |
| Tools/files/forms | Schema/auth, bounded execution, upload/ingestion/retrieval/delete, public form validation, submissions and ownership |
| Widgets/templates | Published-agent selection, domain/token rules, embed lifecycle, template CRUD/copy and permissions |
| Runs/recordings | Correct lifecycle, ordered transcripts, errors, playback/download/delete, access controls and retention |
| Billing/usage | Verified receipts and callbacks, atomic/idempotent credits and debits, actual model/source attribution, plans and ledger/UI reconciliation. Pending security work is not accepted until verified. |
| Reports/admin | Accurate metrics/filter/export, users/workspaces/plans/settings, audit, monitoring and working actions without fake success |
| Operations | Idle CPU near baseline, bounded active CPU, restart recovery, migrations/backups, observability and rollback without affecting other sites |

The separate client-build/superadmin licensing concept remains design-only and must not modify this main product during speech remediation. No destructive crash mechanism. Define enforceable licensing and supported customization boundaries separately.

## 8. 23 October candidate decision and next actions

Kokoro was exercised as an isolated HTTP API on the VPS (2 CPU quota, 1 GiB memory limit). Its Docker image was 505,943,361 bytes excluding mounted weights. Setting ONNX intra-op threads to two and disabling spinning improved the tested HTTP response times, but short Hindi still took 1,672–1,999 ms, longer Hindi 2,523–2,656 ms, Hindi male 2,035–2,083 ms, Roman Hinglish 2,208–2,216 ms and short American English 1,147–1,197 ms to first byte. Full WAV output was buffered. These ten trials do not establish population percentiles or human voice quality.

The separate streaming diagnostic accidentally attempted to load a second model inside the same 1 GiB container and triggered its OOM termination. This is a diagnostic setup failure, not proof that one loaded Kokoro model cannot stream. No streaming latency measurement was obtained. The candidate container and image were removed; the production API health remained OK. Downloaded weights and Antigravity's samples remain available for reference. Kokoro is not accepted for live replacement: the current measured latency and US English identity do not satisfy the user's requirements.

Local catalog changes now allow an actually installed Kokoro server to report its voice/language/streaming metadata instead of being silently excluded by name. Candidate service and catalog checks: 23 passed locally. These changes are not deployed. Tests, benchmark scripts and result files remain local and must not be pushed.

Next: evaluate the published Qwen3.5 2B Q4_K_M against the running Q8 model with identical prompts, thread/context budgets and no thinking; check Hindi/English/Hinglish grammar, grounding and tools as well as first text. Keep IndicConformer/Moonshine and the working Hindi/English TTS until a measured replacement is accepted. Continue full pipeline and platform acceptance; do not delete active services before a viable replacement is connected.

### Qwen Q4 comparison

The official Qwen3.5 2B Q4_K_M was downloaded and compared with the selected Q8 model using identical short synthetic prompts, three inference threads, context 4096, batch 128, seed 42, temperature 0.2 and thinking disabled. Sixteen requests completed. Non-identical warm English/Hinglish/order prompts reached first text in 1,009–1,101 ms on Q4 versus 1,295–1,439 ms on Q8. Identical cached repeats were 103–123 ms on Q4, versus 111–149 ms on Q8; those cached figures are not live caller response latency. Initial model loading added approximately 5.7–5.9 seconds for both. No hidden thinking output was reported.

Both models invented an order status in the no-lookup test; Q4 also produced awkward Hindi grammar. Neither passed the quality gate with that minimal prompt. The real workflow prompt, tools and sustained conversations still need comparison before any selection change. The production Q8 model was restored and confirmed resident after the benchmark. The downloaded Q4 remains an unselected evaluation candidate, not a deployed replacement. Evidence: ignored `run/qwen2b-q4-benchmark.jsonl`.

Admin settings/catalog corrections are in local product commit `8ca1009f` and server source commit `174660e8`. They remove stale fixed-engine claims, move the optional compatibility endpoint under Advanced, and preserve installed TTS voice/streaming metadata. TypeScript validation passed. A rollback-backed UI build is in progress under `/root/kodewaves-rollback/speech-settings-20261023-v2`; source commits alone do not prove the live UI has changed. No tests, diagnostics or this plan were pushed.
