## Current deployment: CPU queue and prompt prefix fixes

The TTS adapter now starts synthesis requests in text order. An isolated three-phrase server check reduced first dispatched audio from 1,398 ms to 393 ms without increasing total synthesis time. This does not prove voice clarity or complete-call latency.

Contact data now follows static language, voice and contact-policy instructions so caller changes preserve more of the cache prefix. A targeted same-model check measured changed-caller prompt evaluation of 9,187 ms before and 1,799 ms afterward. The uncached new-layout prompt still required 11,253 ms; the overall target remains unachieved. Local/server commits: 59fa32e5/e6f55537. All 22 focused tests passed, API health verified after idle deployment. Full-call verification is pending.

Recent user calls selected Qwen3.5 2B Q4. A loaded Q8 snapshot was insufficient evidence of saved workflow selection; future checks must use run-scoped model metadata. Keep model experiments stopped and prioritize the captured delay, cutoff and telephone overlap failures. Browser interaction remains unavailable in Work settings.

## 23 October: current local checkout verification

On the Windows `stabilize` checkout, the local VITS/Piper inventory was corrected to stop exposing Urdu, and detected Hindi/English text no longer silently falls back to a different loaded voice when its matching language model is missing. An attempted 200 ms VAD default for local segmented STT was reverted to preserve the existing 500 ms window. Earlier Python/catalog checks were source-only; this PC has no local Ollama listener or Docker CLI, and the VPS SSH port was unreachable, so neither inference latency nor live audio was verified or deployed.

The local Qwen3 0.6B Q4 workflow probe previously produced faster warm output but invalid Hindi/booking behavior and remains rejected. Qwen3.5 0.8B Q4_0 (563 MB) is a plausible smaller comparison candidate, not a selected model; it must pass the same full workflow grounding and Hindi/English/Hinglish checks before any production switch. Piper's Hindi voices are available upstream, but their model cards cite CC BY-NC-SA dataset terms; do not auto-download or use them in commercial builds without clarified rights. The English voice wired here is `en_US-lessac` (US accent), not Indian English.

### Candidate for Hindi, Hinglish and Indian English TTS

**Highest language-coverage candidate found: Bodhan AI / AI4Bharat Indic-Speak.** Its gated model card claims all 22 scheduled Indian languages plus English, continuous code-mixed speech, shared speaker names for English and Indic voices, and 95 speakers. The card distinguishes 13 production languages from 10 preview languages, so preview output quality is not yet accepted. It is a 3B Llama-3.2-based acoustic model (3.78B BF16 parameters) with a separate vocoder; that is not a lightweight CPU stack. The published ~200 ms first-audio value has no matching 4-core CPU benchmark here and is not evidence for this platform's target. No weights were downloaded because this candidate is gated and its Indic Open Model License prohibits robocalls/auto-dialers and requires prior written permission for hosted APIs serving third parties; that conflicts with Kodewaves campaign/telephony deployment unless the owner confirms a permitted use or obtains a written exception. Treat it as a research lead, not an approved engine. Sources: [Indic-Speak model card](https://huggingface.co/bodhan-ai/indic-speak), [Indic-Speak inference and license information](https://huggingface.co/bodhan-ai/indic-speak#license--terms-of-use).

**MioTTS is not qualified for Indic production.** The cached `run/tts-candidate-samples/mio-cpu-corpus.json` contains 27 labeled engineering prompts, including all 22 scheduled Indian language codes, Indian English, and four code-switched examples. Prompt/output presence alone does not prove model language support or pronunciation quality. The publisher's `MioTTS-0.6B` and `MioTTS-1.7B` cards describe training data as English and Japanese (~100k hours); their reported RTF is measured on an RTX 5090 with vLLM, not CPU. These models therefore do not meet the Hindi + scheduled-language requirement, and their advertised GPU RTF cannot support a CPU speed claim. No Mio model was loaded or promoted. Sources: [MioTTS-0.6B model card](https://huggingface.co/Aratako/MioTTS-0.6B), [MioTTS-1.7B model card](https://huggingface.co/Aratako/MioTTS-1.7B), [MioTTS inference repository](https://github.com/Aratako/MioTTS-Inference).

The author-published `BH-Builds/goonj-1-82M` is a promising CPU candidate: its pinned release describes an 82M-parameter Kokoro fine-tune for Hindi, Indian English and Hinglish, with baked-in male/female personas and an Apache-2.0 model license. The checkpoint is 327 MB on disk, despite its 82M parameter count. Its own README notes synthetic training speech, occasional artifacts, English-based fallback for Hinglish words outside a roughly 300-word lexicon, and a lower naturalness ceiling than larger models. This is not accepted yet: no Windows CPU latency, pronunciation, chunk continuity, or listening test has run. Avoid promotion until its training-data and voice-synthesis rights are checked for client resale. Evidence: [model card](https://huggingface.co/BH-Builds/goonj-1-82M/tree/c1c2caa759fa2b0b3b4159868e4f8c972e66ab5e) and the upstream [Hindi Piper model card](https://huggingface.co/csukuangfj/vits-piper-hi_IN-pratham-medium/blob/main/MODEL_CARD).

Tests, scripts, benchmark data and this plan remain local and must not be pushed.

### Local-PC CPU candidate check (23 October 2026)

On the Windows development PC (Intel Core i5-13420H, 8 cores / 12 logical processors), I ran the existing Qwen3.5 2B Q4 GGUF through the local llama.cpp 0.7.0-dev server using eight inference/batch threads, context 4096, batch 128, no reasoning, and identical concise Hindi/English/Hinglish/grounding/safety prompts. Model-only first-text times were 2,487 ms for the first Hindi prompt with an uncached prefix, then 438 ms English, 469 ms Hinglish, 678 ms grounding and 1,048 ms prompt-injection refusal with the shared prefix cached. Total generation took 2.2–7.0 seconds depending on response length. The replies stayed in the requested language and refused the prompt-injection request, but Hindi booking immediately asked for a phone number before consent, and the no-lookup Hindi answer described the missing booking tool instead of order lookup. This is not acceptable as an automatic production replacement; the test did not include STT, TTS, the full workflow prompt, WebRTC or telephony.

Ollama is a model-serving layer whose current upstream backend is llama.cpp, so replacing Ollama with llama.cpp does not inherently change the inference engine. Direct llama.cpp exposes thread and prompt-cache controls; its server documents prompt reuse as enabled by default. The PC test therefore confirms prompt-cache effects, not that a runtime swap alone beats Ollama. Official references: [Ollama supported backends](https://github.com/ollama/ollama#supported-backends), [llama.cpp server options](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/tools/server/README.md).

Decision: keep the currently configured Qwen3.5 2B selection unchanged. Do not promote a sub-billion model based on speed alone; the 0.6B candidate's existing local benchmark produced broken Hindi and unsafe prompt-injection behavior. Qwen3.5 0.8B Q4 is still an untested candidate (official card: 201 languages claimed; 563 MB Q4), not an approved switch. Its temporary download was stopped because transfer throughput was only about 2 MB/s. Live VPS access was unavailable, so this did not deploy or prove live-call improvement.

### Live TTS phrase-gap regression (23 October 2026)

In `SpeachesLiveTTSService`, the local aggregator previously released a first phrase after only 14 characters and a comma clause after 8. The TTS service awaits each HTTP synthesis generator before processing the next phrase, so a short first fragment creates another sequential request and an audible pause while the following chunk is synthesized. Regression evidence used a Hindi reply from recent call patterns: before the fix it dispatched `ठीक है, तो कृपया` (16 characters) and then the remaining 55 characters as separate requests. The service now waits for a complete sentence or a clause of at least 24 characters at a natural comma/semicolon boundary; it no longer emits the arbitrary 14-character phrase. The same short Hindi reply now dispatches once, and a longer reply splits at its comma. The short-reply regression failed before the change. After both TTS fixes, the combined aggregator, local-model cleanup, interruption, and playback suite passed 68 tests. This reduces request-induced gaps but may delay first audio when the model emits a long unpunctuated sentence. It is source-only and has not been deployed or checked in a real browser/telephone call; actual first-audio time remains unverified.

The same Speaches adapter previously returned silently if a successful response contained no PCM chunks. It now retries that empty-success case once and emits an explicit `ErrorFrame` if the retry is also empty; provider `ErrorFrame`s are passed through without retry. Three focused tests cover recovery, exhaustion and non-retry of real provider errors. This hardens the no-audio path but cannot establish why the live endpoint returns empty bodies without live server logs.

### Faster CPU LLM options and current decision (23 October 2026)

“Ollama” is the model-serving runtime; Qwen is the model. Moving the same Qwen GGUF to `llama-server` is not a model upgrade: both paths use llama.cpp, and the existing local direct-llama.cpp run on Qwen3.5 2B already measured warm first-text at 438–1,048 ms (cold Hindi 2,487 ms). It proves useful prompt-cache behavior, not a guaranteed speedup over Ollama. Keep the live model/runtime unchanged until a same-PC, same-prompt A/B test shows a repeatable improvement. llama.cpp does provide explicit CPU/thread/context/cache controls and a compatible HTTP API, so it is a reasonable runtime to test, not a fix to assert in advance. Sources: [Ollama backend](https://github.com/ollama/ollama#supported-backends), [llama.cpp](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp).

The smaller candidate worth qualifying next is Qwen3.5-0.8B Q4 on the local PC, then on the 4-core VPS if access returns. Its official card claims 201 languages, including multilingual evaluations, but describes the 0.8B scale as suited to prototyping, task-specific fine-tuning, and research/development. That is language coverage evidence, not proof of reliable Hindi booking, English, Hinglish, tool use, or safe contact/consent behavior. A complete local Q4 smoke comparison is now recorded below; it is not qualified for production. The earlier incomplete 89 MB partial was removed. The existing Qwen3-0.6B probe had malformed Hindi and unsafe prompt-injection behavior, so it remains rejected. Source: [Qwen3.5-0.8B model card](https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B).

Other CPU runtimes such as ONNX Runtime GenAI advertise CPU execution and Qwen-family architecture support, but that does not establish a compatible conversion for this exact Qwen3.5 hybrid architecture, faster generation, or comparable Hindi quality. Treat it as a separate engineering experiment only after the small GGUF comparison; do not add another serving stack or download another multi-GB artifact before measuring. Source: [ONNX Runtime GenAI](https://github.com/microsoft/onnxruntime-genai).

Current blocker: the Windows PC has no active Ollama/llama-server process or discoverable llama executable in this checkout, and the VPS health request and SSH port are both unreachable. Therefore no fresh candidate or live comparison could be run in this pass. The next valid comparison must report identical prompt and context, cold and warm first visible token, output tokens/second, full-turn time, peak RAM, and task correctness for Hindi/English/Hinglish plus booking consent/tool grounding. Until then, retain Qwen3.5 2B and avoid a speculative runtime/model swap. These are local findings only and must not be pushed with benchmark scripts or data.

### Auto-language detection candidate check (23 October 2026)

Code-path confirmation: `api/services/configuration/kodewaves_resolver.py` rejects `auto`/`multi`; `api/services/pipecat/service_factory.py` rejects those values again for local CPU IndicConformer; and `api/local_models/indic_stt/app.py` requires a concrete `language` field and only decodes an installed language model. The focused resolver test passes because it asserts this rejection. Thus the UI message is reporting a real missing capability, not a selector rendering bug. The reviewed local container exposes only the 22 IndicConformer language models and no self-hosted English ASR. Adding the 23-label Anuvani language classifier alone would still not make English transcription or Hindi-English code-switching work. Do not enable `auto` until the detector routes to installed ASR models and low-confidence behavior is defined and tested.

The existing local review artifact contains the CIIL Anuvani-LID int8 ONNX model (169 MB), which declares 23 labels: all 22 scheduled Indian languages plus English. Recomputed its checked-in 46-clip selected-reference results: 40/46 correct (87.0%), mean model-only inference 162.9 ms, p95 312.6 ms with two intra-op CPU threads. The simulated narrowband set was 41/46 correct (89.1%), mean 161.6 ms, p95 315.4 ms. These are a small curated subset, not a full accuracy certification or end-to-end STT timing. Failures include English→Maithili (1 s), Hindi→Maithili (2 s), Hindi→English (2.7 s), and Urdu→English/Hindi (1 s); therefore confidence-only routing would still select the wrong IndicConformer language for some real callers. Some synthetic English probes were also classified as Hindi/Telugu. This is not ready to enable as an unconditional production auto mode.

Measured from API handler invocation through completed WAV generation; the model was already loaded, so these are warm utterance timings, not startup or end-to-end speech-call latency:

| Case | Voice | Synthesis | Audio duration |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| Devanagari Hindi | `en_priya` | 1,720 ms | 3.88 s |
| English | `en_priya` | 1,246 ms | 3.02 s |
| Romanized Hinglish | `en_priya` | 1,062 ms | 2.80 s |
| Devanagari Hindi | `en_aman` | 1,307 ms | 3.12 s |

This proves the pinned checkpoint and candidate endpoint can synthesize these sample strings locally. It does not establish first-audio streaming: the handler buffers a complete utterance before returning. It also does not certify accent/naturalness, grammar, pronunciation of names/numbers, telephony codec behavior, sustained calls, or performance on the 4-core VPS. A listening review and full-pipeline timing are still required. Do not claim the 1,000–1,500 ms speech-end-to-audible target is met from these model-only timings.

The candidate contract tests pass (9 tests); API startup and four endpoint calls were exercised directly. The temporary pinned weights, Python 3.11 environment and Hugging Face cache used for this run were removed after recording results. Model/data licensing and resale rights still need review before commercial deployment. ### Small-model CPU comparison on the local PC (23 October 2026)

To test whether a lighter LLM can reduce first-text delay, I downloaded the official 563 MB `Qwen3.5-0.8B-Q4_0.gguf` and ran it, then the existing 2B Q4 GGUF, through the same local `llama.cpp 0.7.0-dev` server build (commit `ceefd16da`), CPU-only, eight threads, context 4096, batch 128, one slot. Both used the same short system prompt and five Hindi, English, Hinglish, booking-consent and refusal probes; this is a smoke comparison, not the complete workflow prompt or live-call pipeline. The shared system prefix remained cached across cases. First pass per-case TTFTs were 236/136/184/188/176 ms for 0.8B (median 184 ms) versus 1,676/462/772/625/700 ms for 2B (median 700 ms). Exact same-input repeats were 66–76 ms (median 73 ms) versus 155–176 ms (median 165 ms). This demonstrates faster model-only first text for this synthetic prompt set, not a guaranteed gain under the full agent prompt or on the 4-core VPS.

The 0.8B model failed correctness: it gave an irrelevant invented “10 minute” instruction for Hinglish booking, produced awkward/non-responsive order answers, and gave a garbled caller-number answer. The 2B model also failed multiple probes by inventing order status, falsely confirming a booking and ignoring a booking refusal. Neither is accepted for production from this test. Keep current selection unchanged while improving prompt/tool orchestration and run a proper full-workflow comparison on identical server hardware. The temporary downloaded Q4 weights and local server process were stopped and removed after the test; no benchmark logs or model artifacts were added to GitHub. Source: [official Qwen3.5-0.8B model card](https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B), [official GGUF files and quant sizes](https://huggingface.co/ggml-org/Qwen3.5-0.8B-GGUF/tree/main).

The same five-probe smoke set was repeated on the existing 2B Q4 GGUF at four threads, with all other server parameters and prompt settings unchanged. First-pass TTFT median was 596 ms at four threads versus 700 ms at eight threads; exact repeats were 168 ms versus 165 ms. Median total generation was 2,986 ms versus 3,147 ms first-pass, and 2,624 ms versus 2,665 ms on repeats. This small local sample suggests four threads reduce contention slightly on this PC, but the warm cached-repeat difference is negligible and does not prove a VPS improvement. The same correctness failures remained. No production CPU setting was changed; the local server was stopped.

### Local Ollama turn-overlap optimization (23 October 2026)

For non-realtime calls using local segmented STT and the local Ollama-marked LLM, the pipeline now starts provisional LLM inference when finalized STT text arrives during Pipecat's existing 600 ms speech-end pause. Pipecat's speculation gate withholds provisional output until the turn is confirmed; resumed caller speech withdraws it, and provisional tool calls are cancelled. Booking/contact deterministic shortcuts are skipped while the gate is speculating, avoiding side effects. Existing VAD stop values, the speech-end pause, external-turn providers and explicit Smart Turn configuration are preserved. This can overlap up to 600 ms of LLM work; it cannot reduce model compute time and is not a guarantee of 600 ms less latency.

Focused local verification passed 71 tests across speculative turn cancellation (including STT/VAD event order and interim transcript invalidation), turn strategy selection, booking tool choice, and TTS phrase aggregation/retry. Ruff checks passed for the new strategy and focused tests. This is not an end-to-end audio/WebRTC/telephone benchmark. Live VPS health/SSH is still unavailable, so deployment and caller-perceived latency improvement remain unverified. Keep this change local until it can be reviewed and exercised through a real call; do not push tests or diagnostic artifacts.

Next's build configuration also skipped type validation and lint, so neither is accepted as verified by this build. ## Shared speech ordering correction and reverted batching experiment

Evidence recorded 23 October 2026, approximately 17:57–18:11 UTC. This section supersedes the earlier 72-character batching deployment claim. Browser/telephone listening acceptance is still open.

A joint replay streamed the actual published Workflow 21 prompt (4,785 characters) through production Qwen Q4 and the actual HTTP TTS endpoint. Both runs produced the same 81-character Hindi reply. These are LLM-request-to-pipeline-PCM timings, excluding STT, VAD, transport and speaker playout:

| Aggregation setting | First model text | First PCM | Estimated playback underrun | Requests |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 72-character batching | 159 ms | 4,738 ms | none in this sample | 1 |
| Earlier 12-character first-sentence threshold | 117 ms | 1,036 ms | 1,060 ms | 2 |

The 72-character setting reduced request count but regressed first audio by about 3.7 seconds. It was reverted. A direct TTS-only first-byte result near 100 ms must never be described as end-to-end voice latency. Neither configuration proves the requested 700–800 ms or 1.0–1.5 s speech-end-to-audible target.

A separate shared runtime defect was reproduced: the asynchronous custom tts_process_generator returned before PCM arrived, allowing Pipecat's following TTSTextFrame to be queued first. Observed pre-fix frame order: TTSTextFrame, TTSAudioRawFrame. That can place not-yet-spoken words into assistant history and make transcript timing lead real audio. The custom background dispatcher was removed; standard ordered HTTP generation already runs independently of downstream playback, and the single-worker speech endpoint cannot parallelize those requests. Language resolution, caching, endpoint selection, voice metadata and turn keepalive remain.

The rebuilt production API uses the restored streaming thresholds (min 24, first 12, comma clause 36) and ordered HTTP generation. Fresh production-container checks passed:
- Delayed audio arrives before its spoken-history text.
- A cancelled synthesis generator closes and emits no stale PCM.
- An empty generator does not report successful synchronous audio.
- Full Pipecat two-sentence pipeline emits Audio, Text, Audio, Text.
- Full Pipecat interruption regression emits old first PCM, interruption, new PCM, new text; old later PCM and unspoken old text are absent, and the next turn completes.

Rollback images: kodewaves-api:rollback-20261023-before-tts-batch (original pre-batching API) and kodewaves-api:rollback-20261023-before-tts-ordering (72-character experiment). Server source snapshot before ordering change: /var/tmp/kodewaves-speaches-tts-pre-ordering-20261023.py. Only the API service was rebuilt/recreated. No tenant model selection, workflow definition/draft, database data, unrelated site, or production UI was changed by this batch.

A temporary Qwen2.5 1.5B candidate (986 MB) was tested with the same real prompt. Cold Hindi first text was 15,463 ms and full response 22,471 ms; its Hindi was ungrammatical and repeated irrelevant content. Warm English first text was 469 ms but total response 3,038 ms. It was rejected and removed, and qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M restored resident. No production model configuration was remapped. A request-only caller-name reminder also failed to correct the unavailable-booking promise, so that speculative instruction was not deployed.

Open requirements remain: actual browser heard-audio acceptance and interruption/naturalness; shared truthful action capability and repeated questions across all agent roles; accurate Hindi/English/Hinglish recognition and real automatic detection; complete male/female/language coverage; live p50/p95 stage timing; telephone acceptance after browser; every broader platform domain. This is a shared ordering correction, not full speech/platform readiness. Inline diagnostics and test audio remained in memory on the VPS; no diagnostic/test/report was committed or pushed.

## 24 October 2026 server replay: clause splitting and action grounding

The API remains healthy with zero restarts. A VPS-only replay compared the deployed TTS sentence aggregator to a candidate that also splits later comma clauses. The test streamed two Hindi responses at a paced character rate through the real Indus gateway and measured first PCM and estimated playout underrun. For the longer order answer, the candidate made three TTS requests instead of two; both variants had zero estimated underrun. For the shorter appointment-style answer, both variants produced the same two segments and zero estimated underrun. An earlier unpaced run had a 7.2-second first TTS request while later requests were fast, so that result is not evidence of a chunking improvement. The extra clause split was not deployed.

A separate replay used the published Workflow 21 prompt, current Qwen Q4, and the real completion adapter. With the existing instruction, an appointment request returned first text at 22.713 seconds and wrongly asked for name to book an appointment even though the node has no booking tool. A request to check an order returned first text at 4.453 seconds and still asked for a name after saying live order status was unavailable. Replacing the no-tool paragraph with a stronger, explicit restriction did not solve either defect: the appointment reply still suggested it could provide an official number, while the order reply invented a WhatsApp bot role and used masculine Hindi grammar. The candidate instruction was not deployed. These timings are LLM request to first text only and vary with cache and CPU load; they do not prove browser/telephone latency.

This evidence rules out a simple clause-threshold or prompt-wording tweak as a reliable fix. The next shared-flow work must ground responses in actual attached tools and caller data, and measure first audio in a real browser turn. The observed cold model delay alone exceeds the 1.0–1.5 second target. No model/runtime setting or production workflow was changed in this replay; all scripts and audio stayed in memory on the VPS.

## 24 October 2026 same-model CPU server comparison

Benchmark on the 4-core VPS only: the official llama.cpp CPU release b11850 served the existing Qwen3.5 2B Q4 GGUF from the Ollama volume on 127.0.0.1:18080 with four CPU threads, 4096 context, one slot. The production Ollama container, models, API routing and Docker services were not changed. Three requests used the published Workflow 21 prompt and real adapter messages; the llama.cpp test merged adjacent system messages because its chat template rejects a second system role. This small prompt-template difference limits direct quality comparisons.

With the appointment request, first LLM text was 4.315 s from Ollama versus 13.685 s on llama.cpp's cold prefix. The next warm Hindi name turn was 5.102 s on Ollama versus 0.563 s on llama.cpp; the English-hours turn was 5.458 s versus 5.246 s. Repeating the identical appointment request three times showed cache effects: llama.cpp first text 0.126/0.426/0.593 s, Ollama 4.458/0.121/0.162 s. Hence the impressive sub-second samples are prompt-cache hits, not reliable live latency. Both runtimes produced the same ungrounded booking question; the serving swap does not fix capability grounding or answer quality. The production Ollama process already runs its own llama-server using the same GGUF, with one 4096-token slot. Merely replacing its wrapper has no demonstrated sustained speed or correctness advantage.

The private benchmark process was stopped and its temporary binary/request files removed from /var/tmp. API health remained healthy with zero restarts. No model/config/UI/workflow change was deployed. This remains a rejection of a runtime-swap shortcut, and real browser/telephone first-audio acceptance is still required.

## 24 October 2026 no-tool appointment grounding

The published Workflow 21 receptionist still has no book_appointment tool, and the CPU model had asked for a name as if it could save a booking. The shared direct-caller path now handles only standalone, explicit appointment-booking requests when there is no usable lookup/action/transition tool. It tells Hindi, English or Roman-Hindi callers that booking is unavailable here and refers them to official support. The same function is used by WebRTC/telephony voice and text chat. When any non-end tool or routing transition is available, the request continues through the configured workflow; combined, negative and unrelated requests are not intercepted. This does not create a booking capability or fix recognition mistakes.

Nine in-container positive/negative/combined-intent checks passed against the bind-mounted production source. Python compile passed. Existing unrelated CRLF whitespace diffs cause repository-wide git diff --check to report pre-existing trailing-whitespace warnings outside this edit. No active workflow run existed in the last ten minutes before the API-only restart. The restarted API returned HTTP 200 from /api/v1/health, Docker healthy, restart count zero. No live browser or telephone call was placed, so caller-heard latency and the full booking flow remain unverified.

## 24 October 2026 automatic language detection feasibility on the VPS

Further model-card screening: Meta's MMS-LID-256 is a 1-billion-parameter model under CC-BY-NC-4.0, so it is neither a light nor a commercially usable default for this resale platform. ARTPARK-IISc's MIT-licensed Vaani-LID_v0 covers 42 Indian languages plus English but uses a Whisper-large-v3-turbo encoder (about 0.8B parameters); its CPU first-turn cost is unmeasured here. Vakgyata-tiny is Apache-2.0, 24M parameters and explicitly covers Indian English, Hindi and ten other Indian languages, but it omits several installed IndicConformer languages. MIRA-IndicLID advertises 22 languages yet classifies *text after transcription*, so it cannot choose the recognizer before transcription. None of these model cards proves reliable short, noisy caller-audio recognition over the full installed inventory. Sources: https://huggingface.co/facebook/mms-lid-256 , https://huggingface.co/ARTPARK-IISc/Vaani-LID_v0 , https://huggingface.co/onecxi/vakgyata-tiny , https://huggingface.co/mira-iitjmu/MIRA-IndicLID . These are screening findings, not deployed changes.

25 October server-only Vakgyata-tiny ONNX probe: the Apache-2.0 24M-parameter checkpoint loaded in 293 ms in an isolated two-core, 1-GiB container. Six short synthetic utterances generated by the production TTS gateway took 71-139 ms each for language inference after resampling to 16 kHz. Hindi, Kannada and Malayalam were classified correctly; Indian English was misclassified as Marathi, Tamil as Malayalam, and Telugu as Marathi. The three wrong predictions each had a displayed top probability of about 1.0. Synthetic TTS voices do not prove real-caller accuracy, but this failure is enough to reject this checkpoint as the production automatic router for the main Hindi/English/South-language requirement. No production configuration changed; the isolated container exited and its temporary 93-MB ONNX weights and metadata were removed from `/var/tmp`.

The installed IndicConformer INT8 STT inventory lists all 22 scheduled Indian languages plus English, but its /v1/audio/transcriptions endpoint requires an explicit language and rejects auto. The existing UI cannot truthfully enable auto for this service without a separate detector and routing. A private, CPU-only feasibility benchmark used official whisper.cpp b5454 and multilingual ggml-tiny. Three roughly two-second Indus-generated 16 kHz samples were Hindi, Indian English and mixed Hindi/English. With four CPU threads and detection only, results were Hindi p=.594 in 560 ms, English p=.949 in 528 ms, and mixed as Hindi p=.693 in 597 ms. These are synthetic voices, not caller recordings or an accuracy evaluation. The detector alone consumes over half of the requested 1.0–1.5-second total response budget before IndicConformer, Qwen and TTS, so simply running it serially is not an acceptable latency fix. Its all-22-language reliability is unproven.

The next implementation must constrain auto to a measured language set, test real accented/short/code-switched caller audio, and consider parallel speculative recognition to hide detector delay. No auto option was enabled or presented as working from this feasibility check. The temporary model, binary and generated clips are not production dependencies.

## 24 October 2026 appointment phone display audit

The appointments page renders `customer_phone` from the API verbatim. The API returns the linked CRM contact's `phone` column verbatim; appointment persistence strips whitespace but does not generate, pad, or mask digits. A read-only production query of seven recent organization-1 appointments found two contacts with a +91-length number containing four repeated zeros or ones in the stored value and five without that pattern. The two patterned contacts were created at appointment time on 15-16 October, not by a UI display formatter. Their exact numbers were not copied into diagnostics. Do not rewrite stored phone numbers or reject repeated-digit numbers automatically, since legitimate numbers can contain repeats. The origin of those two inputs remains unverified; check the original authorized call/form or campaign row before correcting the CRM contact. This finding does not prove campaign CSV import is sound in all cases.
Follow-up: both patterned appointment contacts were new rows created in the same transaction as their appointments. A read-only exact-value comparison against all two present campaign queued-run phone values found no match. This supports appointment-time entry as the immediate stored source, not the current campaign queue; it does not establish what a caller originally said or exclude an earlier deleted upload. No CRM data was changed.
## 24 October 2026 browser audio run 394 and exact help correction

Run 394 was a real browser microphone/WebRTC test of Workflow 21. Server events show the first final Hindi STT text `अः मुझे एक हेल्प चाहिए था`; the next finals were fragments `हाँ मुझे एक`, `मुझे ऑर्डर कर रहा था`, and `बुत अभी तक उसका कुछ पता`. The bot repeated a generic help reply on the first two; this is failed conversational acceptance, not a passing Hindi call. For the first response, STT final was at 06:41:16.382 UTC, LLM first text at 06:41:21.572, TTS first audio at 06:41:22.406; the pipeline's measured user-to-bot latency was 11.7665 seconds. Later cycles measured 4.2025, 2.8948 and 3.2310 seconds. TTS first-byte metrics were cache hits (roughly 0.001-0.006 seconds) and do not measure physical playback gaps. WebRTC later logged repeated no-audio-frame timeouts and ended at 06:42:13 UTC with `Consent to send expired`; recording/transcript artifacts were saved. English/Hinglish and telephony were not accepted.

The production `direct_caller_reply` handler was already wired into both voice and text chat; the local checkout had fallen behind the server copy. The exact transcript failed its Hindi help pattern because `अः` was missing from its hesitation prefix. Added only that variant to the live server, validated the exact transcript returns a help clarification while an incomplete fragment and an unrelated request do not, and restarted only the API after confirming no unfinished runs in the preceding ten minutes. Health recovered to HTTP 200, Docker healthy, restart count 0. This corrects one reproduced trigger but does not solve STT fragmentation, reply repetition for all inputs, response-time target, or WebRTC transport failure. Synced the five affected current server source files back to the local checkout without pushing tests or reports.
Follow-up waveform check of run 394 was performed entirely inside the VPS API container; temporary WAV files were deleted immediately. The saved user and bot tracks are 75.4 s at 16 kHz. They contain simultaneous energy during several two-second bins (including around 0-4 and 20-24 s), but zero-lag PCM correlation was near zero; this is not evidence of copied bot audio into the microphone. The user track includes quiet intervals and speech-like bursts, but simple RMS cannot reliably identify speech end or distinguish browser packet loss from an intentional pause. Do not turn the saved STT request start timestamp into a speech-end timestamp or claim precise end-to-ear latency from this recording alone. The browser call remains failed acceptance pending a measured follow-up.
## 24 October 2026 language and prompt budget follow-up

Run 394 LLM usage reports 3,037 prompt tokens, 2,530 prompt-cache-read tokens, and 90 completion tokens. The Start Call node prompt in workflow 21 is 1,933 characters before runtime policy/persona text and tool schemas. First-turn model TTFB was 5.182 s; later measured LLM TTFBs were 0.986, 1.602, and 1.647 s. This points to first-turn LLM/prompt processing as a major delay alongside the first STT segment; a smaller model or reduced prompt should be compared with the same task/accuracy before changing production.

The live IndicConformer endpoint requires a language form field and loads one language-specific CTC recognizer per request. Its OpenAI-compatible route rejects `auto`; service_factory also rejects auto for the local IndicConformer path. Automatic routing is therefore not a selectable setting the current self-hosted recognizer can perform. A detector/recognizer cascade or another multilingual local STT model is needed, and its cost must be included in speech-end-to-first-audio.

## 24 October 2026 Qwen repetition-penalty and prefix-cache check

A private VPS-only exploratory comparison sent three short Hindi, English and Hinglish requests through the configured Qwen3.5 2B Q4 endpoint with current frequency/presence penalties (`0.5` / `0.3`) and with both set to zero. The saved Workflow 21 prompt plus standard action-grounding, no-tools and local caller-response instructions totaled about 939–948 input tokens. The current-penalty requests reported only 376–436 cached tokens and first-text times of 5.4–6.4 seconds. The immediately following zero-penalty requests reported 935–944 cached tokens and first-text times of 0.12–0.16 seconds. Because prefix-cache state differed, those timings cannot show a penalty speedup; the cache hit itself accounts for the large timing difference. The zero-penalty replies remained poor or irrelevant in Hindi/Hinglish. English also lacked the production request's clear-English hint in this probe, so its language mismatch is not a valid live-pipeline comparison. No penalty or runtime setting was changed.

This provides evidence that cold prefix evaluation can dominate the first visible text, while near-complete prefix reuse cuts model-only first-text delay sharply. It does not prove a usable warmup strategy or the same cache behavior on a real audio call. Next latency work should compare stable-prefix reuse or prefill timing on the actual first WebRTC turn while measuring CPU contention; do not claim sub-second speech response from these text-only cache-hit samples. All probes were ephemeral and no benchmark script, output log, or report was pushed.

## 24 October 2026 repeated contact-question regression

In the live Workflow 21 Test Chat, `मुझे नया ऑर्डर करना है।` produced a combined name/mobile question. The caller answered `राहुल`, and the model repeated that same combined question word for word. The visible transcript confirmed a new user turn had arrived; this failure was a repeated assistant response after a short slot answer. It was not evidence of an audio echo.

Added `caller_name_followup_reply` to the shared contact-context helper and wired it into both the text-chat turn runner and the voice LLM wrapper. It activates only when the immediately preceding assistant message asked for both a name and phone/mobile number, and the latest caller answer is a short name-shaped phrase without digits. It then acknowledges the name and asks only for the remaining number. Negative answers, numeric phone answers, longer unrelated messages, and turns without that prior combined question remain on their existing paths. The helper also saves the caller-supplied name in turn-scoped contact state.

Focused local verification passed 36 tests across the new contact-context cases, voice LLM routing and confirmed booking, with one existing Pipecat deprecation warning. Ruff passed for the new helper and focused test file. Docker health is `healthy` with zero restarts and the public API returned HTTP 200. A fresh in-container functional check passed Hindi and English name-answer cases. ## 24 October 2026 prompt-cache fix and production follow-up replay

A live browser voice test (run 399) reproduced the user-visible follow-up defect. The first Hindi order clarification arrived quickly; the address-change follow-up showed `LLM first token: 18,797 ms`, and the next follow-up showed `6,601 ms`. The answer did not handle the requested address change accurately. VITS returned HTTP 200, but the API logged `SpeachesCachingTTSService` timing out while canceling a TTS task when subsequent STT audio arrived. This confirms that the speech task can be interrupted during incoming speech; the exact playback gap and whether any segment was lost still need a PCM/client-side trace.

Root cause for avoidable Qwen follow-up prefill was found in `booking_tool_choice.py`: the local-model response guard was inserted directly before the newest user message, so its location shifted deeper on every turn and Ollama could not reuse most of the prior prompt. Run 399 reported 793 cached tokens from 2,659 prompt tokens. Moved the guard into a fixed system-prefix position and made its language direction static (Hindi, English or natural Hinglish), so the prior conversation prefix remains unchanged. Before the patch, a server-side builder reproduction shared only 2 messages between first and follow-up requests; after the patch, all 4 first-turn messages remain an identical prefix. A server-side functional assertion and Python compilation passed. The API container was restarted; it returned healthy state, restart count 0, and HTTP 200 health checks.

Production text-chat run 400 verified the deployed builder and current model path. The first Hindi address-change turn took `response_ms=15,857 ms`; the second took `9,232 ms`. Stored usage shows 1,287 prompt / 35 completion tokens and no cache read on the first turn; the second used 1,355 prompt / 34 completion tokens, including 775 cached tokens. Prefix reuse is now working, but the 9.2-second second turn shows model token generation is still too slow for the requested 1.0–1.5-second target. Both replies were generic and did not properly guide the address-change flow. This remains an open product behavior and latency issue.

Server-only candidate checks: Gemma 3 1B Q4 produced 0.64–0.88-second responses on short prompts but answered a Hindi address follow-up in English; it was removed. Llama 3.2 1B produced 1.33–1.44 seconds on short Hindi/English prompts, but switched to Hindi for an English request and gave a poor Hinglish answer; it too was removed. Qwen3 0.6B and Qwen3.5 0.8B had already failed quality checks. The configured Qwen3.5 2B Q4 model was restored and warmed; no STT/TTS model or container was changed.

Open acceptance: measure post-fix first token on a warm real voice follow-up; add exact PCM-first-byte and browser playback timing; diagnose whether the cancellation timeout and separate TTS requests cause the reported stop/play gaps; correct address-change behavior based on available workflow tools; verify English/Hinglish generation; and run the same test over authorized telephony. These results do not establish the 1.0–1.5-second voice target or general production readiness. All candidate models were removed after testing; no benchmark scripts or reports were pushed.

## 24 October 2026 per-utterance TTS output tracing

Production call events previously captured only TTS started/stopped boundaries and one first-audio event per turn. They did not preserve PCM chunk counts or associate audio bytes with each TTS context, so the saved run could not distinguish gaps in synthesis output from gaps later in transport or browser playback.

Added per-context counters to the production call-event recorder. Because Pipecat reuses one audio context across all sentence-level synthesis requests in an assistant turn, that event now records context ID, first-PCM delay, synthesis wall time, PCM frame count, byte count, calculated audio duration, maximum gap between PCM frames, count of gaps over 750 ms, and whether no audio arrived. The call summary also records how many TTS contexts remained open when the call ended. It stores no PCM or transcript text. The update is deployed on the production API branch. Host Python syntax compilation and a post-restart in-container smoke check passed; a synthetic 802 ms PCM gap was recorded; API container is healthy with restart count 0.

This enables the next call trace to distinguish a server-side PCM underrun from audio arriving continuously at the server, but it does not itself fix the user-heard playback gap and it does not measure browser playout. No new browser or telephone call has run since deployment, so there are no post-change latency or uninterrupted-audio acceptance results. Qwen response delay, browser playback timing, telephony continuity, and the 1.0–1.5 s target remain open.

## 24 October 2026 bounded streaming for later TTS phrases

The run 399 recording and production source review showed that the configured aggregator emitted its initial clause, then held later LLM text until a sentence boundary (`streaming_phrase_characters=None`). That can leave synthesis requests separated by multiple seconds while the LLM continues generating. Enabled bounded later-phrase emission at 48 characters in the production Speaches adapter, keeping the current Hindi/English voice route and first-clause threshold unchanged. This lets the streaming synthesis endpoint prepare the next phrase while the current audio is playing.

Verification: production Python syntax compilation and `git diff --check` passed. An in-container aggregator smoke check on a Hindi follow-up split the response into three ordered chunks and reassembled the original text exactly. Restarted only `kodewaves_api`; it returned healthy status and `/health` reported status `ok`. This is a targeted buffering change, not proof that caller-heard pauses are gone. The next fresh WebRTC call must check `max_pcm_gap_ms` and caller playback; telephony continuity and end-to-end latency remain open.

The API startup health probe briefly returned 502 during the restart, then recovered after startup completed.

This will distinguish TTS synthesis gaps from gaps at the server output-transport boundary on the next real WebRTC call. It does not see RTP packet arrival or browser/telephone playout, and no fresh call has yet exercised the new event fields. Existing model/voice selections and UI were unchanged. No test script, audio or report was deployed or pushed; a pre-change source backup is kept in `/var/tmp` for rollback.

## 24 October 2026 production Qwen request-budget and cold-start correction

Production code review found that local Qwen's configured `max_tokens=192` was not an effective output cap: the OpenAI adapter omitted the `max_tokens` sentinel when serializing the request to Ollama. The local Ollama path now sends native generation options (`num_predict=48`, `num_ctx=4096`) and a valid long keep-alive (`8760h`) in the request body. These changes are isolated to the marked local-Ollama Qwen path; cloud providers are unchanged. The first attempted keep-alive value `"-1"` was rejected by Ollama's OpenAI-compatible endpoint as an invalid duration; it was corrected to `"8760h"` and verified by Ollama's process inventory showing the model remains resident for 12 months. The API image was rebuilt and only `kodewaves_api` restarted. Its container is healthy with zero restarts and the public API health endpoint returns HTTP 200. No model, STT, TTS, voice, database, or tenant selection was changed.

Verification: the focused Qwen request-budget, tool-routing and TTS aggregation suites passed locally (62 tests, one existing deprecation warning). A live in-container service-builder assertion confirmed the outgoing body includes `think=false`, `num_predict=48`, `num_ctx=4096` and `keep_alive=8760h`. A live OpenAI-compatible Ollama request returned streamed text, and `api/ps` confirmed the model's expiry is 12 months away. This prevents model unload/reload after idle and enforces a real output-token cap; it does not reduce warm-model CPU generation time to the 700–800 ms goal or fix poor Hindi grounding. Direct live prompts measured warm first text between about 0.34 and 1.15 seconds and whole generation between 1.3 and 4.3 seconds; Hindi samples sometimes generated off-topic content. Thus sub-second end-to-end response and acceptable multilingual accuracy remain unachieved.

The earlier real Workflow 21 run 399 logs contain a WebRTC `MediaStreamError` that cleared the inbound audio track and failed output writes. This is evidence of a transport failure in that call, not proof that all TTS clipping is caused by WebRTC or that it is fixed. The new numeric PCM/output-frame traces have not yet captured a fresh real call. Fresh browser voice and authorized telephony acceptance remain open. No full-workflow/model quality acceptance is claimed, no test script or report was deployed, and nothing was pushed.

## 24 October 2026 regional TTS stream completion fix

Found a thread-safety defect in the production regional TTS gateway: PCM chunks were put on an `asyncio.Queue` through the event loop, but the worker thread's completion callback inserted the end-of-stream sentinel directly. `asyncio.Queue` is not thread-safe. A missed wakeup could leave the HTTP PCM stream open after the model had emitted its final chunk, delaying completion/stop signaling and contributing to stuck or chopped playback. Changed the callback to enqueue the sentinel through `loop.call_soon_threadsafe`.

Source commit `610d35ec` was pushed to `codex/production-integration` and fast-forwarded on the VPS. Before the TTS restart, both API workers had zero active calls. Rebuilt and restarted only `kodewaves_indic_vits_tts`; API, UI, STT, Ollama, Indus model service, configuration and volumes were untouched. The new regional gateway image is `sha256:7657362410bfd34c6dcfb632d1fb3a6f8fedc19d8458c957a942fd77470ca266`, healthy with zero restarts. A model-free worker-thread smoke emitted two PCM chunks and closed the stream within the two-second deadline. A real short Hindi/English mixed synthesis through the live gateway returned its first PCM in 150 ms, finished in 2,057 ms for 4,880 ms of audio, and had an 81 ms maximum HTTP read interval; this measures server synthesis/streaming, not browser, WebRTC or carrier playout. Gateway memory was about 250 MiB, and API/UI remained healthy with zero restarts.

This fixes one concrete unsafe completion handoff; it does not establish that every reported mid-speech cut is fixed. The 2B Qwen still causes multi-second first-text waits on real turns, phrase-to-phrase generation can leave gaps, and client/telephone playback remains unverified. A fresh live caller test must correlate the existing `voice_tts_trace` and `voice_output_trace` gap metrics with actual heard playback before claiming the cutting/stopping issue is resolved. The 1,000–1,500 ms response target and 700–800 ms stretch target remain unmet.

## 24 October 2026 Workflow 21 LLM prefill diagnosis

Read the active draft definition (41) from the VPS database. Its Start-node prompt is 1,933 characters and has no action tools. A representative prompt composed from that node plus the production shared guidance and Hindi speech rule was 4,502 characters. The selected resident CPU model `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M` reported 1,053 prompt tokens, 11.245 s prompt evaluation, 11.318 s to first visible text, 2.620 s output generation and 13.936 s total for a synthetic Hindi room enquiry. Model load was only 1 ms. A second turn sharing the prefix had 1,117 prompt tokens, 0.971 s prompt evaluation, 1.004 s to first text and 3.845 s total. The model's answers still greeted again and invented details about room services. The first probe wrapper exited with a here-document terminator error after both requests had returned final Ollama metrics; no model request failed.

To test whether prompt size alone could meet the target, used the same saved workflow prompt plus a concise 438-character platform guard. This cut the candidate prompt to 522 tokens and reduced uncached prompt evaluation to 5.469 s and first text to 5.594 s, with 9.470 s total generation. The answer invented cloud testing and a transfer that this workflow cannot perform. Its follow-up to an English-language request answered mostly in Hindi and asked about departments; despite 0.932 s first text, it failed language and grounding quality. This candidate prompt was **rejected and was not deployed**. Both comparisons ran only on the VPS with zero active calls; the current model, prompt, organization settings and services remained as configured.

These numbers distinguish model loading from prompt evaluation: on this 4-core VPS, the long first-turn wait was overwhelmingly prefill, while cached-prefix follow-ups were faster. Even the shorter tested prompt missed the requested first-audio target before STT and TTS, and degraded answer correctness. The next optimization must preserve actionable workflow context while avoiding repeated prefill or bypassing generation only for reliably recognized narrow intents. No sub-second general voice-turn claim is supported.

## 24 October 2026 run 399 stage and recorded-audio diagnosis

Read the persisted numeric event timings for production Workflow 21 run 399 and measured the stored bot recording in-place in the API container; transcript text and audio were not printed or copied to the PC. Run 399 recorded STT TTFB values of 1.194 s, 0.349 s and 0.400 s. The local Qwen 3.5 2B Q4 LLM took 18.797 s and 6.601 s to first token on two caller turns. TTS first-PCM TTFB was 0.546 s and 0.851 s on those turns. This establishes that LLM waiting dominated the long reply delays; the TTS request itself began returning PCM in under one second in those two cases.

The bot WAV is a 16 kHz mono recording collected downstream of the WebRTC output transport, with temporal silence padding. Its length is 85.44 s. Threshold-based numeric inspection found a 3.46 s silence gap between two active bot-audio spans in one response. The API log also shows two successful VITS HTTP requests in that response 4.729 s apart. These facts support sequential phrase synthesis waiting for additional generated text as a contributor to the stop/start sound; they do not identify whether all of the 3.46 s was spent generating text or waiting in a transport queue. The same run logged two initial failed output-frame writes and later a dead inbound WebRTC audio track. The existing failed-write warning does not say whether the peer was disconnected, the output track missing, or the track queue rejected the frame.

Next diagnostic requirement: add safe numeric-only logging of connection-ready state and output-track presence for failed SmallWebRTC writes, correlated with the per-turn PCM/output trace. A fresh real WebRTC call must capture these fields to distinguish a model text starvation gap from a transport gap. Do not respond by removing sentence streaming wholesale: that would trade audible gaps for a longer first-audio wait. Telephony has no equivalent end-to-end evidence yet. No service changes were made in this diagnosis, no transcript/audio was exported, and no diagnostic artifacts were retained or pushed.

## 24 October 2026 SmallWebRTC failed-write reason tracing

Added throttled, numeric-only logging in `SmallWebRTCClient.write_audio_frame` for rejected frames: the reason (`closing`, `not_connected`, or `output_track_missing`), connection/closing/track-presence booleans, and rejected-frame count. A successful write after rejection logs recovery and resets the counter. Logs are limited to one warning per second during a continuing failure, and contain no audio or transcript. Production Pipecat source differed from the local file, so the server change was applied only to the two matching code anchors rather than copying over the server file; a pre-change copy is retained at `/var/tmp/smallwebrtc_transport.py_pre_audio_reject_trace_20261024.py`.

Verification: local `test_smallwebrtc_transport.py` passed all 10 tests; Ruff and diff checks passed. A production-container smoke check exercised both a `not_connected` rejection and subsequent successful write/recovery, and confirmed the expected warning/info logs. Only the API image was rebuilt and `kodewaves_api` recreated; it is healthy with zero restarts and the public health endpoint returns HTTP 200. This instrumentation has not yet captured a fresh real WebRTC call, so it diagnoses the next failure but does not claim the current caller-heard WebRTC cutout is fixed. No tests, logs, audio, transcript or reports were pushed.

## 24 October 2026 explicit reply-language request conflict

Production Workflow 22 run 402 recognized the caller's Hindi question asking to speak English, then recognized their name. Its Qwen replies stayed in Hindi and repeated a prior request for the name. The existing request-only language hint labeled the switch question as Hindi and instructed Qwen to answer in Hindi, directly conflicting with the caller's explicit language choice. Corrected that hint to prefer an explicit English/Hindi reply request and retain it for later utterances in the same detected language; a later utterance in another language resumes automatic matching. The change does not alter stored transcripts. A production-container check covered the switch, subsequent Hindi name, unchanged prior hint, and return to automatic Tamil. The API rebuild completed with health 200 and zero restarts. Rollback copy: `/var/tmp/caller_language_context.py_pre_explicit_switch_20261024`.

This verifies request construction, not an actual improved reply. A fresh WebRTC call must confirm Qwen follows the switch and remembers the name. The repeated answer in run 402 may have additional causes in workflow context or model behavior, so it is still open. The 700–800 ms full speech target and caller-heard audio continuity also remain unverified and unmet in the prior measured runs.

Follow-up server replay with Workflow 22's saved prompt and the run 402 turn sequence showed Qwen3.5 2B still emitted the earlier Hindi refusal after the corrected request language hint was deployed. A stronger hint and removal of the repeated earlier assistant replies each produced inconsistent or unrelated responses; prompt-only nudges are not sufficient evidence of reliable language and name handling on this CPU model. Do not claim the language switch is fixed from the request-construction check. Reconsider the model/runtime and conversation architecture before more prompt tweaks. The replay made no workflow writes or phone calls.

The same replay demonstrated a separate concrete truncation: the 48-token Ollama `num_predict` cap ended a short appointment reply with `done_reason=length`. Raised that bounded cap to 80 in the production request adapter, retaining `num_ctx=4096` and `think=false`. The production adapter now builds an 80-token request; the API is healthy with zero restarts and public health 200. Rollback copy: `/var/tmp/booking_tool_choice.py_pre_80_token_cap_20261024`. This removes one known cap-induced cutoff, but may increase generation time for long replies and does not prove the reported playback pauses are fixed.

Text encoding was verified through SSH: Devanagari code points arrived intact. A short supplied-name history sent to the selected Qwen3.5 2B Q4 returned a refusal to recall the supplied name (first text 1635 ms, wall 4048 ms, 116 input tokens). Explicit known-name guidance did not repair it. Removing sampling penalties also failed. Both chat rendering and manually rendered ChatML returned the same contradictory answer on an English-only name question, weakening a renderer-only hypothesis. The installed Q8 copy also failed that exact question (10016 ms wall including 6083 ms loading). A thinking-enabled Q4 probe spent 200 generated tokens / 13048 ms entirely on reasoning with no visible answer; it was not adopted.

Temporary official `qwen2.5:1.5b` candidate (Ollama Q4_K_M, 986MB) was tested only after active call count was zero. With a short receptionist system prompt: supplied-name memory returned correctly in 3524 ms first text including 2500 ms loading; Hindi 494 ms first text /1945 ms total but merely paraphrased the request; Hinglish 746/1445 ms asked again for the already supplied name and date; correction 555/959 ms used corrected name Ravi but asked again for the supplied date. Candidate rejected for context/slot correctness, unloaded and deleted. Selected `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M` restored (HTTP200), `ollama ps` confirmed CPU residency and4096 context. No persisted model setting changed, no caller call was initiated, and no benchmark ran locally. These synthetic probes are not full live-flow acceptance or a guarantee of model-only speed under the real prompt.

Official candidate references: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and https://ollama.com/library/qwen2.5:1.5b. Upstream cache issue reports concern other configurations and do not establish a cause on this VPS; no runtime upgrade was justified by those reports alone.

### Caller name memory fix deployed: 24 October 2026
Root cause confirmed: generic caller name statements were not saved in call state unless they answered the specific combined name-and-phone prompt. Qwen was also shown to contradict names present in history, so relying on the model to recall them was insufficient. Added shared call-state capture for explicit English/Hindi name statements, caller corrections, and compound requests; direct name-recall questions now use the stored value in both live calls and text chat. Model requests also receive a concise reminder when a name has been explicitly supplied. Focused contact/language checks: 50 passed. Server source branch advanced cleanly from 521e2f62 to ea273dff with only three tracked product files. API image sha256:915afb12a86fb33d3bade5c1bbc3f491a597cbd9dcf7f872b6936ba01f0181e4 is running healthy, zero restarts. Live container smoke captured आदर्श from a compound Hindi request and answered "What name did I give you?" with the stored name. Ollama remains on qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M at 100% CPU, 4096 context. No tests, diagnostics or reports were committed. This verifies the specific name recall path; broad context retention, other answered slots, TTS continuity and end-to-end latency remain unaccepted.

### Name-only caller answers: 24 October 2026
Extended name capture for the common case where the assistant asks “What is your name?” and the caller answers with only a short name. It requires a prior assistant name question, rejects digits and common yes/no responses, and keeps the result in the same call-scoped state used for direct recall, caller corrections and text chat. 52 focused contact/language checks passed. Source commit d209d5a2 is on the server branch; API image sha256:67d118e501e28e58277071cb6fb8c9e842f29a8aeddac12cdd2d61ea766f3d98 responds healthy with zero restarts. A live container smoke confirmed name-only Hindi capture and later direct recall. This does not resolve model forgetting of dates, booking intent, speech gaps or the latency objective.

25 Oct 2026 spoken auto-LID candidate audit (research only; no service changed): AI4Bharat IndicLID is text language ID, not raw-audio LID (https://github.com/AI4Bharat/IndicLID). Project Vaani's published Vaani-LID_v0 covers 42 spoken languages but is 0.8B parameters, too large to assume it meets the 4-vCPU latency target without isolated benchmarking (https://vaani.iisc.ac.in/models). SpeechBrain VoxLingua107 ECAPA is Apache 2.0 but its published labels omit several scheduled languages and its own card cautions on foreign accents and small-language accuracy (https://huggingface.co/speechbrain/lang-id-voxlingua107-ecapa). Anuvani-LID's 14 Oct 2026 paper reports 115M parameters and 22 scheduled languages plus Indian English with calibrated results, but no verified public weights/runtime were found, so it cannot yet be installed as a production dependency (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20263). Bodhan Indic-Transcribe Core has integrated auto-LID/ASR for 25 Indian languages, but is a 1.2B-class model; its 4-vCPU per-turn latency and licensing/deployment contract are not validated here (https://github.com/Bodhan-AI/bodhan_genai). The earlier 24M ONNX Vakgyata-tiny candidate failed 3 of 6 synthetic speech classes despite 71–139 ms inference. Keep explicit selected decoder as the verified local path and do not represent it as automatic detection. Next viable evaluation requires a released full-coverage lightweight checkpoint or a carefully measured multilingual ASR replacement, with real held-out phone clips and CPU p50/p95 before enabling auto.
## 25 October 2026 review of the attached latency scan

The attached scan is useful as a ranked hypothesis list, but several items describe an older code state. Read-only production verification on `codex/production-integration` at `241ef53f3` found the selected `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M` resident on CPU with a 4,096-token context and `Forever` keep-alive; request code also supplies `num_ctx=4096`, `num_predict=80`, and disables Qwen thinking. Therefore the scan's proposed P3 context/keep-alive change is already present in the active path and is not currently a proven root cause. The local TTS adapter already uses a 200 ms PCM chunk, so P10 is also implemented. The prompt composer only appends a live clock for date-sensitive workflows; it does not change that system prefix every turn in ordinary workflows.

The strongest unclosed scan hypothesis is single-slot Ollama contention: voice calls default to background variable extraction on node transitions, and non-Dogra provider runs reuse the conversation LLM for extraction; node transitions can also start background context summarization. Because production runs one Ollama parallel slot, these requests can queue ahead of the next live answer. This is a plausible explanation for slow follow-ups, not yet proven for a current call. Do not disable extraction or summarization globally: they can affect workflow variables and long-call context. Next evidence needed is a fresh server call with per-request LLM queue/wait timings tagged by operation (`conversation`, `variable_extraction`, `context_summary`) plus the run/node IDs; then apply a narrowly scoped scheduling change if contention appears in that trace.

Current server snapshot during this check: Ollama 2.484 GiB / 4 GiB, API 974.9 MiB / 2 GiB, IndicConformer 1.345 GiB / 2 GiB, regional VITS 249.8 MiB / 1.5 GiB, Indus TTS 889 MiB / 2 GiB; all measured CPU usage was idle-level (<1%). The tiny warmup container was running at 12.72 MiB and 0% CPU. This snapshot shows memory residency, not the reported call-time CPU spike; it does not justify deleting active models or containers. Production branch and head matched the source checkout; no production settings or containers were changed during this investigation.

## 25 October 2026 bounded local-TTS follow-up deployment

The scan's prior-phrase recommendation was inconsistent with current source: `streaming_phrase_characters` had been set to `None`, which held later local speech until a sentence boundary after the initial clause. The earlier 48-character configuration had previously been reverted after a live gap/naturalness check, so it was not restored unchanged. Set later phrase release to 64 characters to avoid tiny restarted clips while allowing long replies to start the next TTS request before sentence completion. The first-clause threshold remains 36 characters; voice IDs, Indus/Inaya routing, STT and LLM settings are unchanged.

Commit `1e2f2cf0` contains only `api/services/pipecat/speaches_tts.py`; it was pushed to `codex/production-integration` and fast-forwarded on the VPS. The previous API image (`sha256:adc01701f6bde5225e7edbfe473059e8bca9e0e90e692795982d61e92ecccf17`) was tagged `kodewaves_api:rollback-241ef53f-20261025`. Only the API service was rebuilt/recreated. In-memory syntax parsing succeeded; an isolated Hindi aggregation smoke emitted 48/67/66/8-character pieces and retained the complete ordered reply. In-container construction of the deployed Speaches adapter confirmed `streaming_phrase_characters=64` and `first_clause_characters=36`. API health recovered to HTTP 200 / healthy with zero restarts; active-call count was zero at restart. No tests, reports or scripts were staged or pushed. The local pytest invocation could not load `api/conftest.py` because this Windows worktree has no `api/.env.test` (`DATABASE_URL` was unset), so the new test file was not treated as passing evidence.

This is a targeted streaming change, not proof that audible gaps, natural prosody, cutoffs, or response latency are fixed. The latest stored Workflow 21 run remains 407 (24 October); no fresh caller turn exists after deployment. Inspect adjacent TTS PCM and transport gap traces from the next WebRTC/authorized phone call before deciding whether to tune or revert the threshold. Do not claim the 700–800 ms target is met.

## 25 October 2026 stable prompt-prefix deployment

The contact reminder in `BookingOpenAILLMService.build_chat_completion_params` appended the caller's currently known name/phone to the *first* system message. That changed the prompt's earliest tokens whenever those details were captured, invalidating Ollama's longest reusable prefix and forcing CPU prefill over the conversation again. Removed the per-turn name/phone injection and extended the stable local response guard to tell Qwen to use contact details already present in the transcript without asking for them again. Caller transcript history and booking's call-scoped contact state remain intact; booking consent remains separately enforced.

Commit `ef35ed8e` contains only `api/services/pipecat/booking_tool_choice.py`, was pushed to `codex/production-integration`, fast-forwarded on the server, and deployed by rebuilding/recreating only `kodewaves_api`. Previous API image was preserved as `kodewaves-api:rollback-1e2f2cf0-20261025`. The in-container request-shape smoke confirmed that the configured first system prompt is unchanged, caller details remain in the user transcript, and no dynamic caller contact reminder is injected. The new API image is `sha256:0e874e6735ce8467f26c50f982f09c9e430b0fa004588913fefc996fc30dc1b4`; internal health returned `status: ok`, Docker reports healthy and zero restarts. No live caller turn was available after deployment, so reduced LLM prefill/TTFB and improved follow-up behavior still require a real WebRTC or authorized telephone measurement. ### 25 October 2026 TTS stream probe and Qwen CPU-thread comparison

With zero fleet active calls, three direct HTTP PCM requests to the production TTS gateway produced Hindi, Indian-English and Hinglish samples without writing audio files. First bytes arrived in 370/257/223 ms; total synthesis took 2.12/1.88/2.02 s for 4.60/4.03/4.52 s of audio. Maximum gap between HTTP chunks was 120/170/230 ms. This shows that those single-request sample streams were continuous at the gateway layer; it does not measure gaps between separately synthesized phrases, transport/browser playout, pronunciation quality, or caller-heard audio.

Also compared Ollama `num_thread=2` vs `3` on the resident Qwen3.5 2B Q4 with three short Hindi/English/Hinglish prompts. Generation throughput increased from 17–19 to 22–25 tokens/s, but sample first-text latency varied widely (2 threads: 1.39–11.02 s; 3 threads: 0.93–5.66 s) and both settings produced the same incorrect English/Hinglish response, including an invented 10 a.m. booking. This small direct-prompt probe is too noisy and fails quality; no thread option or production setting was changed. The remaining evidence priority is one end-to-end call measuring phrase-to-phrase/output playout gaps and Qwen prefill after cached turns.

### 25 October 2026 production trace and thread-budget follow-up

After commits `d30278de` (stable, shorter local voice prompt) and `64d1082b` (configurable local Ollama thread budget, default 3), the VPS source branch `codex/production-integration` is at `64d1082b7`. Only the API container was rebuilt for these changes. Production API health returned 200 and Docker reported healthy with zero restarts; the Ollama, IndicConformer STT, regional VITS and Indus TTS containers also remained healthy with zero restarts. An in-container request-builder smoke confirmed the actual Qwen3.5 Ollama payload contains `num_thread=3`, `num_ctx=4096`, `num_predict=80`, disabled reasoning and the existing indefinite keep-alive. `ollama ps` confirms the selected Qwen3.5 2B Q4 remains resident on CPU at context 4096. No fresh user WebRTC/telephone turn was present after deployment, so these code/runtime checks do not establish a live-call speedup.

Latest full live baseline remains Workflow 22 run 408: STT final was logged at 07:16:03.916 UTC; the following LLM stream produced first output after 11,450 ms (`prompt_tokens=1,314`, `cached_prompt_tokens=512`, `completion_tokens=22`, `system_chars=5,438`, two tools); first TTS PCM followed about 0.95 s after first LLM text. The speech-energy observer saw first audible speech 15.308 s after turn start. TTS PCM trace reported first PCM at 206 ms, full wall time 1,830 ms, maximum gap 634 ms, `no_audio=false`. This points to LLM prefill as the main delay in that run; it does not prove the current 3-thread or shorter-prompt deployment improved it. The 700–800 ms goal remains unmet by a large margin.

A bounded synthetic, same-prompt context comparison used 1,705 prompt tokens and `num_thread=3`: context 2,048 prompt evaluation 16,798 ms; context 4,096 prompt evaluation 15,637 ms. Both requests reported 4–5 seconds of model-runner load; the runner had been restarted after an earlier overlong probe, and the cause of the second request's load time is unverified. These reload costs make this an inconclusive comparison, not a controlled latency result. The roughly 7% difference is not a meaningful controlled latency win and lowering context risks clipping multi-turn history; production remains at 4,096. The overlong probe was stopped; a short non-empty smoke then restored Qwen3.5 2B Q4 as a warm resident model. The two speech services, STT and API stayed healthy. No candidate, context, voice or routing changes were made as a result.

Next acceptance evidence must come from a new user-started WebRTC turn: correlate STT final, LLM first text, TTS first PCM and first speech-energy sample for the same turn, and capture subsequent-phrase PCM gaps plus run status. A new production `llm_stream_trace` should also distinguish prefill from generation. Until that run exists, latency, no-cut playback, multi-turn memory and telephone behavior remain open. Do not claim the target is achieved.

### 25 October 2026 server-only Gemma 3 1B CPU candidate qualification

Google's official Gemma 3 card lists multilingual training across 140+ languages, and the Ollama registry lists `gemma3:1b-it-qat` as a 1.0 GB Q4_0 checkpoint with 32K context. That justifies a VPS smoke test, not an assumption of reliable Hindi/Hinglish. Sources: https://ai.google.dev/gemma/docs/core/model_card_3 and https://ollama.com/library/gemma3:1b-it-qat .

After the protected fleet counter reported zero active calls, pulled the candidate to the production Ollama store without changing saved configuration or routing. On five short, identical synthetic Hindi/English/Hinglish/memory/grounding prompts using three CPU threads and 4,096 context, Qwen3.5 2B Q4 first visible text was 1,088–1,591 ms and total responses 2,143–3,543 ms. Gemma's cold first case was 3,357 ms; its four warm first-text results were 123–252 ms and full responses 453–1,458 ms. These prompts had only 85–137 tokens and are not comparable to the 1,314-token full live request; they do not establish live-call speedup.

Qualifying Gemma with the actual Kodewaves grounding, language, voice-persona and contact instructions still failed critical requirements: it falsely confirmed an unbooked appointment, changed a known caller name to `कोडवेवज़` and asked for it again, and produced repetitive/awkward code-switched Hindi-English. It did honor an explicit English switch in one sample. Candidate rejected; the 1B model is not selected. Removed `gemma3:1b-it-qat` from the server. Sent a non-empty warm-up request to the unchanged `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M`; verified it is again the sole resident model at context 4096 with long keep-alive. API, Ollama, IndicConformer, Rasa VITS and Indus TTS containers are healthy with zero restarts. No production model selection, provider route, TTS, STT, or workflow settings changed. Commercial license review was not performed.

This confirms a speed/quality tradeoff but provides no acceptable replacement. The production target and model correctness remain open; next candidate must be evaluated with full workflow prompts, tool schemas, multiple turns, and a user-started end-to-end voice turn before any selection change.

### 26 October 2026 server voice probe — synthetic, not live-call acceptance

On the production VPS, confirmed protected active-call count was zero before running probes. With the selected Qwen3.5 2B Q4 model and 3-thread/4096-context defaults, a fresh short two-language/hotel prompt run produced Hindi first text at 2,633 ms and first PCM at 3,504 ms; English first text at 1,160 ms and first PCM at 1,899 ms; Hinglish (Romanized Hindi) first text at 1,261 ms and first PCM at 2,015 ms. A simulated playback queue showed a 520 ms Hindi underflow on that initial run; English and Hinglish had none. This queue model is not a WebRTC/audio-device measurement. The Hindi LLM response also invented room availability despite a prompt instruction not to invent it.

Repeated direct TTS calls to the production gateway using the production `X-Kodewaves-Speech-Language` header then showed Hindi first PCM at 160–250 ms, synthesis 1,395–1,596 ms for 2.49–3.23 seconds of audio, with measured delivery deficits no greater than 160 ms. Indian English first PCM was 301 ms and synthesis 2,704 ms for 4.96 seconds of audio, with a 181 ms maximum delivery deficit. These calls did not overlap Qwen generation and do not prove that live TTS never stalls. The discrepancy suggests CPU contention and/or model warm-state affects the first concurrent Hindi turn; the exact cause is not yet isolated. Both TTS containers have startup warm-up code, but runtime call traces were not gathered here.

Current evidence: warm model-only short probes can be fast, but first-turn Hindi/English speech remains above the 700–1500 ms target; the full-workflow prompt, actual recognition, audio playback, telephone path, hallucination and occasional audio interruption remain unverified/open. Do not claim the issue fixed. Next isolate cold versus warm concurrent LLM+TTS on the production gateway while recording per-stage `voice_stage_trace` and PCM arrival/playout, then adjust CPU scheduling or buffering only from that reproduction. No settings or routing were changed by this probe; scripts and outputs stayed outside GitHub.
