### 25 October 2026 Workflow 22 prompt root-cause probe

Read the exact definition bound to live run 409. Its start-node prompt is 1,982 characters and repeats language and booking instructions; the composed system prompt is 4,909 characters. The live LLM trace for run 409 had 1,086 prompt tokens, zero cached, two history messages (65 characters), and two configured tools. Thus caller history was not the source of the large first-turn prompt. The prompt is chiefly workflow and platform instructions.

On the VPS, with zero active calls and the current 2B Q4 model, compared that composed prompt with a nonpersisted 642-character rewrite of the node prompt. The rewrite cut synthetic prompt tokens from 1,309 to 1,037. In one Hindi request with the production-like transition tool offered, first text fell from 12.24 s to 9.55 s (prefill 12.18 s to 9.46 s), but the rewrite gave a poor Hindi answer. On an English switch, the original answered correctly in English; the rewrite incorrectly invoked the finish-call transition and produced no spoken text. This rewrite is rejected and was not saved or deployed. The one-request timings cannot establish an expected end-to-end improvement.

An earlier synthetic two-tool probe offered the booking tool before caller confirmation. Production's request wrapper suppresses that tool until confirmation, so the empty outputs in that probe are not evidence of a production empty-response fault. The follow-up probe used only the actual finish-call transition condition. Current production workflow definition and model routing remain unchanged.

25 Oct 2026 unchanged-prompt prefill probe: A nonpersisted system-only Ollama call with the exact 4,909-character Workflow 22 prompt, the actual finish-call transition schema, and a one-token generation took 4.53 s (prompt eval 4.47 s). The subsequent synthetic English caller request still took 4.80 s to first text (prompt eval 4.78 s) and incorrectly answered in Hindi. The prefill did not demonstrate the needed first-turn speed or language reliability, so no prefill behavior was wired into production. This probe reused a warm runner and possibly part of its earlier prompt cache; it is not a cold-call or WebRTC acceptance. Current workflow definition, server model selection, and code are unchanged.

### 25 October 2026 first local speech buffer deployment

Run 409 links audible stop/start to two short sequential local TTS requests. The gateway logged two HTTP speech requests per reply on the affected turns; `voice_tts_trace` recorded incoming PCM gaps of 1,171 ms and 756.7 ms, while paced `voice_output_trace` showed internal silence of 560 ms and 320 ms. On saved replies, the local text aggregator would divide four of five short responses into two synthesis requests. Joining the full reply would increase time to first audio, so commit `88541e52` adds a 400 ms PCM content buffer only for the first segment of each `indic-vits-rasa-13` turn, including cache-hit delivery. It preserves frame order and releases short/error responses without loss.

The exact candidate module was staged in the VPS API container and exercised without model inference: PCM ordering, hold-until-buffer, short-answer flush, error order and one-time turn claim all passed. No test or report was committed. The server branch fast-forwarded to `88541e52`; the prior branch head `167f4aaf` is retained as `codex/rollback-pre-tts-buffer-20261025`, and the old API image as `kodewaves-api:rollback-20261025-pre-tts-buffer`. The API image rebuilt and only the API container was recreated after active calls were zero. It is healthy with restart count zero; HTTP health is 200 and the running image exposes the 0.4 s buffer value. No fresh caller audio after this deployment has yet measured whether the output silence fell; this is a deployed hypothesis, not full WebRTC or telephony acceptance.

Run 410, which began at 08:55:24 UTC, overlapped an earlier Ollama load probe. Its trace has six STT-final stage events, one accepted user transcription, no LLM-first-text event, and user hangup before a reply. The overlap makes the probe a plausible source of contention, but the logs do not prove sole causation. Direct inference/load probes on the live Ollama service have been stopped. Run 410 must not be counted as an unbiased platform latency or empty-response acceptance result.

25 Oct 2026 automatic recognition audit: The installed `indicconformer-ctc-int8` service advertises its installed language decoders in `/v1/models`, but `api/local_models/indic_stt/app.py` requires a `language` form field on every transcription and explicitly does not identify spoken language or decode mixed-language speech. `api/services/catalog/catalog_service.py` therefore correctly sets `supports_auto_detect: false`; `ui/src/components/AIModelConfigurationV2Editor.tsx` hides Auto for that model. The missing Auto option is a real capability gap, not a UI-only defect. No separate local acoustic language identifier is present in this service path. AI4Bharat IndicLID identifies *text*, so it cannot route audio before recognition. A CPU INT8 faster-whisper detector is a plausible separate stage (upstream supports language detection), but its accuracy for all 22 installed decoders, short noisy calls, added latency, and commercial model terms require validation before it can be wired or advertised. No production setting or model was changed by this audit. Sources: https://github.com/AI4Bharat/IndicLID ; https://github.com/SYSTRAN/faster-whisper/blob/master/README.md .

25 Oct 2026 live transcript preservation: The WebRTC UI hook previously removed *every* non-final user transcription when a new STT event arrived. If an earlier interim never received a final event, a later caller turn deleted that visible history. Commit `aac02af9` replaces only the current interim utterance before the latest bot/user turn boundary, leaving older caller messages visible. Only two product UI files were committed; existing tests and reports stayed untracked. The branch was pushed, fast-forwarded on the VPS, and the UI image built successfully. Server rollback branch `codex/rollback-pre-transcript-20261025` and prior UI image `kodewaves-ui:rollback-20261025-pre-transcript` were saved. The UI-only container was recreated: new image `sha256:b29fff14d8e6ca22798ae2dc7802ea17ed494739f3bbb98e8bc42326ed60c0ac`, healthy, zero restarts; public workflow route responds 307 to unauthenticated request and API health responds 200. The production Next build explicitly skipped type validation/lint. No authenticated browser call has yet verified the visible history behavior, and this UI change does not prove that the LLM retains prior messages internally.

25 Oct 2026 post-deployment observation: Production API and UI remain healthy, with zero active calls and no voice-stage logs since the UI deployment. The local browser surface is disabled by Work settings, so an authenticated WebRTC call could not be started from this session. Read-only code tracing confirms local VITS emits callback audio while each phrase is synthesized, but Pipecat awaits each phrase request before processing the next; `LocalSpeechAggregator` can split a reply at its first phrase and again near 64 characters. This supports the earlier sequential-request gap diagnosis. The 400 ms first-audio buffer may cover part of that gap while adding up to 400 ms to initial playback; only a real post-deployment voice call can establish its net effect. Do not claim the TTS interruption issue is fixed from the deployment alone.

### 26 Oct 2026 production STT recognition smoke check

Queried the live STT `/health` and `/v1/models`: IndicConformer is loaded and advertises auto detection for 23 installed codes. Then generated short Hindi and Indian-English samples with the already-running TTS container and posted them in-memory to the production STT transcription endpoint, once with `language=auto` and once pinned. Hindi auto-detection returned `hi` at 0.9727 confidence and transcript `नमस्ते मुझे एक कमरा बुक करना है`; pinned Hindi returned the same transcript. English did not pass: the generated English sample was classified as Hindi at 0.7566 confidence in the repeated comparison, producing Devanagari transliteration; with language pinned to `en`, it returned `When Dito Bukarum.` rather than the intended sentence. The first independent English sample had been classified `en` with confidence 0.5368 and returned a semantically similar but different sentence, demonstrating instability across the TTS sample.

Conclusion: service availability and Hindi smoke recognition are verified, but STT cannot currently be described as working fine across Hindi and English Auto mode. The sidecar's Auto flag confirms a language identifier is loaded; it does not guarantee reliable Indian-English classification or recognition. This is synthetic TTS audio only, not human microphone audio, code-switching, all languages, WebRTC, or telephony acceptance. No production configuration was changed during this probe.

### 26 Oct 2026 CPU English decoder comparison

Compared the installed Moonshine-tiny English decoder and an isolated, disposable `faster-whisper base.en` INT8 candidate (74M parameters, 2 CPU threads, 2 CPU limit) on the same short platform-generated Indian-English clip. In this repeat the Auto LID selected English (confidence 0.7304); Moonshine took 96 ms in pinned-English mode and returned `Where I need to book a room`. The candidate took 911 ms warm and returned the same text; its cold model load was 5,002 ms. This is a single synthetic sample, so it does not establish WER or Indian-accent quality. It provides no evidence to replace the much faster Moonshine decoder with Whisper base.en on this CPU. The candidate container was `--rm`; it exited cleanly, and the production API/STT/TTS/Ollama containers remained healthy with no config changes.

Together, repeated synthesis of the same English prompt yielded inconsistent Auto language decisions (Hindi at 0.7566 on one generated clip, English at 0.7304 on another); the text transcription also varied. The next necessary acceptance input is natural caller speech, especially Indian-accented English and Hindi-English code-switching. No inference fix is being claimed from synthetic TTS clips.

Research surfaced Indic-Mio-LiteRT as a plausible single-engine candidate: its public model card says it covers all 22 scheduled languages plus English and code-mixed text, with Apache-2.0 LM weights and MIT codec. Built the existing committed sidecar as a benchmark-only image on the VPS and launched it on the Kodewaves Docker network, limited to 2 CPUs and first 3 GB then 4 GB RAM, with a dedicated temporary model volume. It OOM-exited during token-step graph initialization at both limits before serving health or audio. Removed the temporary container, model volume (2.8 GB), and benchmark image; production API, STT, both current TTS containers, UI, and Ollama remain healthy. No routing/configuration changed. Candidate rejected for the current server resource budget; no audio quality/latency figures were produced.

Indic-Speak was also ruled out for this target based on its published 3.36B parameter size and greater-than-two-minute single-shot generation. DhVaani's advertised 27-language coverage is an unbenchmarked alternative, but its model repo requires access/terms acceptance and depends on reference-voice assets; it is not approved or installed. The unresolved production need is a verified all-22 CPU TTS that fits the current resource envelope while retaining acceptable Hindi/English quality and consistent voice identity.

### 26 Oct 2026 Anuvani detector accuracy and code-switch limitation

Reviewed the current `api/local_models/indic_stt/app.py` path against the Anuvani-LID paper. This deployment runs one utterance-level 23-way LID decision, then chooses one language-specific IndicConformer or Moonshine decoder for the entire clip. It does not identify language spans inside a Hinglish utterance. This is consistent with the paper's limitation that code-switched utterances need frame-level language diarization rather than a single utterance label. The paper reports 0.897 macro-F1 across the 23 language classes, 0.901 for Indian English, and 0.84 for Hindi on its speaker-disjoint set; it reports Hindi-variety/Hinglish confusion, including Hinglish clips labeled Urdu or English. Source: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20263 (evaluation and limitations sections).

The existing synthetic production English probes are a concrete observed failure, not proof that the detector is globally unusable: their English Auto labels varied, including Hindi. The next valid accuracy gate is a small fixed evaluation using licensed natural Indian-English, Hindi and code-switched audio, pairing the same clips with LID label/confidence, transcript, and stage latency. No production inference change was made from this paper review. Indic-Transcribe advertises multilingual code-mixed ASR and integrated LID (https://ai4bharat.iitm.ac.in/static-blogs/bodhan-asr/bodhan-asr.html), but its published 1.2B size and recorded-audio inference make it an unbenchmarked accuracy candidate, not a proven low-latency replacement for the current 30M IndicConformer decoders.

### 26 Oct 2026 clean public human-speech STT smoke

Used five 6–8 s FLEURS validation WAV demos published in the Anuvani-LID repository. Its `samples/README.md` attributes the recordings to Google FLEURS and CC-BY-4.0; no customer audio was used. Each clip was posted in memory to the live Indic STT `/v1/audio/transcriptions` endpoint in Auto mode. Auto selected the correct language on all five: Hindi (`hi`, confidence .8445, 1,275 ms), Bengali (`bn`, .9977, 1,850 ms), Tamil (`ta`, .9976, 1,966 ms), Telugu (`te`, .9948, 1,675 ms), and Marathi (`mr`, .9791, 1,740 ms). Each request also returned a transcript, but no FLEURS reference transcript was paired, so this is not a WER/word-accuracy result. These clean, read, single-language examples do not validate Indian-accented English, Hinglish, all 23 labels, noisy speech, WebRTC, or telephone input. The observed Auto+decode latency (1.28–1.98 s per 6–8 s clip) also exceeds the requested 700–800 ms target. Both common Hindi and English decoders were reloaded into the bounded cache after the sweep. Production active-call telemetry was zero before the sweep (loop lag p95 1.76 ms, max 2.14 ms); production containers remained up. No config or routing was changed. Demo source: https://huggingface.co/CIIL/Anuvani-LID/tree/main/samples (Apache-2.0 model repository; sample WAVs separately attributed to FLEURS CC-BY-4.0).

### 26 Oct 2026 DhVaani suitability review (no gated access accepted)

The DhVaani-0.5 card describes 123M parameters, 491 MB F32 weights, 27 languages, 3–10 s single-speaker reference audio plus transcript, 8-step faster mode, and rough code-switch prosody. It exposes full-waveform `synthesize` and does not document a first-audio streaming API. The current official model page labels the repository `cc-by-nc-4.0`, which makes it unsuitable for Kodewaves commercial deployment. The repository is gated behind accepting contact-information sharing; access was not accepted. Source: https://huggingface.co/ARTPARK-IISc/DhVaani-0.5 .

An ungated community ONNX export of the same base model is tagged Apache-2.0, lists 13 language tags, and provides 227 MB of INT8 encoder/decoder/vocoder files; its page claims about 4x real-time on two CPU cores without a reproducible benchmark method. Because it redistributes a conversion of the underlying DhVaani weights, its own metadata alone does not resolve the upstream CC-BY-NC conflict or establish commercial rights. Source: https://huggingface.co/Bbkblo/DhVaani-0.5-ONNX . Neither artifact was downloaded or deployed. The model is not approved as a commercial replacement; a non-commercial CPU benchmark would still not resolve its first-audio streaming, mixed-language prosody, full coverage, reference-voice, or target-latency gaps.

### 26 Oct 2026 Workflow 22 fresh WebRTC run 413 latency and multilingual failure evidence

Inspected production logs for Workflow 22 run 413, matching the user-provided Urdu/Hindi/English transcript. Production source hashes for `speaches_tts.py`, `local_speech_aggregator.py`, `indic_vits/app.py` and `indus_tts/app.py` match this worktree for the inspected files. Run 413 used `indicconformer-ctc-int8`, `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M` and `indic-vits-rasa-13`. The workflow run completed, but the WebRTC output track disconnected at 06:39:01 UTC.

The first slow reply's UI label is confirmed by server trace: Ollama first output was 5,787 ms after request start (total model stream 7,087 ms; 1,050 prompt tokens, 4,365 system characters, four tools). Later first-output times were 885 ms, 2,024 ms and 1,237 ms, so latency is variable and not consistently below 1.5 seconds. A later turn used 1,293 prompt tokens and ten history messages, indicating context and prompt size remain material on CPU.

TTS returned audio, but turn 7 had first PCM at 1,422 ms, 4,460.8 ms synthesis wall time, 3,022.4 ms maximum PCM gap and one gap over 750 ms; the output trace measured 1,760 ms maximum internal silence. Turn 9 had a 1,352 ms maximum PCM gap (640 ms output silence). Other turns were smoother (for example, turn 5 max PCM gap 403.7 ms). This reproduces the user's audible stop/start as a production defect, not a missing-model response. Two `SpeachesLiveTTSService` task-cancellation timeouts also appeared during the call. At call end, the WebRTC track disconnected and the output transport rejected a frame.

The supplied transcript shows Hindi, Urdu and mixed-script caller turns receiving Hindi replies. Current Anuvani routing selects one language label per STT request; it does not provide span-level Urdu/Hindi/English switching. The exact recognized text and language labels for each phrase were not persisted in the API logs, so the precise per-phrase misclassification is not proven by this trace.

No production setting or code was changed for this capture. Next fixes must address the measured first-output stall (prompt/context and CPU-bound 2B model), determine why TTS synthesis has multi-second inter-chunk gaps, and keep turn handling correct for mixed-language caller input. Any change must be measured on a fresh caller-heard call and preserve complete text. This single run is not full acceptance, and the 700–800 ms total response target is not met.

### 26 Oct 2026 production Hindi/Indian-English/Hinglish TTS route smoke

From the API container, made three sequential, small PCM streaming requests through the live `indic-vits-rasa-13` endpoint, which routes Hindi, Indian English and mixed Hindi-English to the installed Inaya engine. No audio files or test scripts were saved. Hindi: first chunk 256 ms, 2,078 ms total synthesis, 2,880 ms audio, max chunk-arrival gap 283 ms, longest below-threshold quiet run 180 ms. Indian English: 275 ms first chunk, 2,444 ms total, 3,200 ms audio, max arrival gap 298 ms, quiet run 350 ms. Hinglish: 294 ms first chunk, 1,744 ms total, 2,830 ms audio, max arrival gap 207 ms, quiet run 310 ms. These three short isolated requests did not reproduce the multi-second output gap seen in run 413; they do not establish human naturalness, telephone playback, long replies, concurrency, or consistent performance. This narrows the TTS defect to a live-turn/longer generation or streaming/playback interaction rather than every short synthesis. No production config or code changed.

### 26 Oct 2026 production STT Auto decoder-swap OOM and bounded-swap fix

After confirming the protected active-call count was zero, ran a sequential in-memory TTS→STT Auto round trip across the 15 currently advertised TTS locales plus Hinglish. Docker events recorded the Indic STT container being OOM-killed (exit 137) during language changes; the API client then saw connection resets/refusals. The source `_load()` constructed a new language decoder before evicting the least-recently-used decoder, temporarily allowing a third recognizer while the configured cache limit is two, alongside the resident LID session. This is the direct resource-overrun path reproduced by the sweep.

Changed `_load()` to evict and release the least-recently-used recognizer before constructing a replacement. A one-off AST harness verified the constructor sees only one resident when adding a third language and the cache stays at two. Saved the previous source at `/root/kodewaves-rollback/stt-bounded-swap-20261026/app.py.pre` and tagged the previous image `kodewaves-indic-stt:pre-bounded-swap-20261026`. Rebuilt and recreated only `indic-stt` after the active-call counter returned zero. New image `sha256:fc2c60a6bc8a0026c620d1e0c8de6ecf00ce4d77eb014fc98bec17db1c4c60d0` is healthy with zero restarts; public API health is 200. Repeating the complete 15-locale-plus-Hinglish round trip completed without a new restart; STT memory was 1.119 GiB / 2 GiB afterward.

Synthetic Auto results: Assamese, Bengali, Bodo, Hindi, Kannada, Maithili, Malayalam, Marathi, Nepali, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Tamil, and Telugu labels matched their source locale; Hinglish was labeled Hindi, which is the expected dominant-language route for the installed utterance-level detector. Dogri was mislabeled Hindi, and the Indian-English voice sample was mislabeled Telugu. STT returned transcripts for all completed samples, with some wording errors. TTS produced 2.0–3.2 s WAVs in 0.856–1.488 s for the 14 regional/Hindi/English routes; Hinglish synthesis took 1.320 s for 2.56 s audio. These are synthetic round trips, not human accuracy, pronunciation, naturalness, WebRTC, or telephone acceptance. Auto detection is therefore not yet reliable enough to claim complete; Dogri and Indian-English are confirmed misses, and fresh caller speech remains required. The 700–800 ms end-to-end target remains unmet.

### 26 Oct 2026 live caller audio and natural English Auto control

Checked run 413's private user-only WebRTC recording (`recordings/413/user.wav`) in memory; no audio was written or included in the report. It is 106.1 s mono 16-kHz audio. A 200-ms energy segmentation found long and short speech intervals. For diagnosis only, a 16-window probe split the long intervals into 5-second windows and posted those plus shorter intervals to live STT Auto. Labels across windows included Urdu (3), Marathi (2), Hindi (2), English (5), Telugu (1), Malayalam (1), Nepali (1), and one empty result; observed per-window latency ranged from 7 ms to 1,727 ms. These windows cut across speech and are not valid utterance ground truth. The transcript also contains Hindi/Urdu and code-mixed script, but it has no timestamps or per-utterance detected-language fields, so exact error attribution for each phrase is not possible. This does show that forced short windows inside a long mixed utterance produce unstable language labels; do not apply them as a production fix.

As a natural-English control, streamed one CC-BY-4.0 Google FLEURS `en_us` test WAV from the public archive directly into memory. Auto detected English at 0.7952 confidence in 1,685 ms; pinned English took 291 ms and returned the same sentence, with number normalization differing from the reference transcript. This is general US English, not Indian-accented English. Together with the wrong Telugu label on synthetic Inaya English, the evidence points to a voice/accent-specific weakness plus utterance-level code-switch limitations, rather than a universal English-classification failure. A fresh actual caller turn after the deployed `local_stt_result_trace` change is still needed to capture one-to-one STT label, LLM language, TTS route and first audio. Neither this STT control nor any of the synthetic probes establish the end-to-end latency target. Source: [Google FLEURS dataset](https://huggingface.co/datasets/google/fleurs).

### 26 Oct 2026 per-phrase live TTS timing deployed

The earlier Workflow 22 run 413 measured a 3,022 ms incoming PCM gap and 1,760 ms output silence, while short isolated gateway requests did not reproduce it. The Pipecat local TTS service awaits each phrase generator before handling the next one, so the missing distinction is time waiting for the next LLM phrase versus time spent synthesizing that phrase. Added `local_tts_phrase_trace` at the service boundary for both cached and uncached paths; it records context, text length, language, first PCM wait, wall time and PCM byte/frame counts without logging transcript text. Local `py -3 -m py_compile` and `git diff --check` passed. With the protected active-call counter at zero, saved the prior server file at `/root/kodewaves-rollback/tts-phrase-trace-20261026/speaches_tts.py.pre`, copied the changed file to the server checkout, and restarted only `kodewaves_api`. The service became healthy with zero restarts, its `/api/v1/health` returned 200, and the module imported in the running container. No model or route was changed. A new real call is still needed to produce phrase traces and decide the actual continuity fix; this deployment itself is diagnostic, not a speech-gap repair.

### 26 Oct 2026 Workflow 22 run 416 and turn-interruption change

The user completed a new Workflow 22 WebRTC test. `local_stt_result_trace` recorded the same call switching short STT segments between Marathi, Urdu, Hindi, Malayalam and Tamil. The UI screenshots show the corresponding mixed-script caller bubbles, Marathi assistant replies, repeated questions, and one partly confirmed reply. The saved 173-second `recordings/416/user.wav` was read in memory on the VPS; no local recording copy was created. Replaying affected windows through Auto and pinned Hindi demonstrated that the same spoken Hindi could decode as Urdu in Auto but readable Hindi when pinned. The first LLM reply showed 6.31 seconds to first output after a partial 7.29-second request; later warm replies were ~0.83–1.51 seconds. Per-phrase TTS tracing found a 4.413-second first PCM wait on one Marathi phrase; other measured phrases started in ~178–469 ms. Turn 13's incoming PCM gap was 974 ms, while several output traces show normal PCM pacing but large waits before first speech. The API logs also show repeated `SpeachesLiveTTSService` cancellation timeouts during the caller's short fragments. These observations refute any claim that Auto, latency, or uninterrupted speech is accepted.

A trial limiting STT Auto candidates to the 15 active TTS languages was built and replayed, but it still mislabeled an affected Hindi phone-number phrase and the Indian-English synthetic control as Telugu. The trial was immediately rolled back using `/root/kodewaves-rollback/stt-candidates-20261026/` and the previously tagged STT image. The restored container image `sha256:fc2c60a6bc8a0026c620d1e0c8de6ecf00ce4d77eb014fc98bec17db1c4c60d0` is healthy, zero restarts. Candidate-list filtering alone is insufficient; no forced Hindi fallback was accepted because that would break real regional-language callers.

For the independently observed repeated cancellations, local segmented STT now starts an interruptible caller turn only after a final transcript, rather than on raw VAD activity or interim text. Other STT providers retain their prior strategies. A direct strategy smoke in the production API environment checked the local final-transcript selector and the unchanged standard selector; the old strategy first failed the check as expected. The prior server source is saved at `/root/kodewaves-rollback/local-turn-start-20261026/run_pipeline.py.pre`. With zero active calls, only `kodewaves_api` was restarted; it became healthy with zero restarts and returned HTTP 200. This is a targeted interruption policy change, not full caller-heard acceptance. A new WebRTC and telephone run must confirm that TTS finishes and legitimate barge-in still works. The run 416 language error needs a stronger detector/utterance strategy before Auto can be accepted.

### 26 Oct 2026 Hindi/fragment language-routing correction deployed

Replayed the stored Workflow 22 run 416 caller WAV directly against the production Indic STT service in memory; no audio was copied to the PC and no transcript text was emitted to the terminal. Auto labeled two longer Hindi windows as Urdu at confidence .8382 and .7786. Short VAD fragments also varied: Urdu .5604/.86, Gujarati .4082, Tamil .4583. This shows both Hindi/Urdu confusion and unstable low-confidence labels on short phone-number speech; increasing the silence window alone did not resolve the labels.

Added per-call STT language continuity in `api/services/pipecat/speaches_stt.py`: map the unwanted Urdu route to Hindi, retain the last reliable language when Auto confidence is below .65, and still accept a clear high-confidence language switch. The normalized language is also placed on the returned transcription result so downstream turn routing sees the same choice. The trace now records detected label, confidence, route decision, and selected language without transcript content. Local Python compile and diff checks passed; local focused route smoke passed. After confirming zero active calls, backed up the server file at `/root/kodewaves-rollback/language-stability-20261026/speaches_stt.py.pre`, deployed to the existing production checkout, restarted only `kodewaves_api`, and confirmed API HTTP 200, running state, zero restarts. The same five route cases passed in the production container. This improves Hindi continuity and rejects Urdu routing, but it does not prove live caller-heard correction, recognition accuracy for every supported language, or uninterrupted TTS; a fresh Workflow 22 call is still required for acceptance.

### 26 Oct 2026 language-aware decoder candidate replay and production verification

The first API-only language-routing patch would have changed the LLM/TTS route after recognition but left the underlying STT words decoded with the wrong language. It was extended before acceptance: the STT endpoint now accepts an optional call-scoped `fallback_language`, maps the unsupported Urdu Auto label to the Hindi decoder, and uses the prior stable language decoder when confidence is below .65. The API sends that fallback from the per-call STT service and returns the selected decoder plus raw detector label/confidence for non-text tracing.

Built an isolated candidate image on the VPS and tested it against the existing model volume. On run 416 audio, a 2.3-second Hindi clip was labeled Urdu at .8382 but selected the Hindi decoder and produced Devanagari output; a 5.57-second phone-number clip was labeled Marathi at .3447 but selected the Hindi decoder when passed the call fallback. The OpenAI Python client multipart request also successfully carried `fallback_language` to the endpoint. Candidate memory was 733 MiB under its 2-GiB limit; candidate was removed after validation. Recreated only the production `indic-stt` service with the tested image, then updated/restarted only `kodewaves_api` with zero active calls. Production replay returned the same two selected decoders and route reasons; API and STT health are up with zero restarts. API route helper smoke passed in the running production container. This verifies saved-clip decoder routing, not a fresh live conversation or caller-heard TTS continuity. The API rollback file is `/root/kodewaves-rollback/language-stability-20261026/speaches_stt.py.pre-decoder-fallback`; the STT pre-change source and old image are preserved in `/root/kodewaves-rollback/stt-decoder-stability-20261026/` and the `rollback-stt-decoder-stability-20261026` image tag.

The production trace review also found an earlier Indian-English control labeled English at confidence .5368. To avoid holding that known English label in Hindi, the deployed policy uses a .50 confidence gate for English and .65 for other labels. Focused production API routing checks verified English .5368 switches to English, while weak Tamil .4583 stays with the prior Hindi route and Urdu .8382 goes to Hindi. This policy change was included in the later STT image/API deployment. No fresh Workflow 22 call trace has appeared after that deployment yet (server active-call count remains zero), so caller-heard continuity is still unverified.

Added narrowly scoped, unambiguous phonetic digit aliases for the observed Gujarati and Tamil forms. The existing call-fragment state machine still requires a complete 10–15 digit number and does not infer unclear words.

Local Python compilation and an isolated exact transcript replay passed: seven digits followed by three more collected as `9538300941`. The container is healthy with zero restarts and `/api/v1/health` returns `{"status":"ok"}`. Replayed the same two-part mixed-script number inside the running API container; it passed. This addresses the observed digit-recognition fallback only; fresh caller-heard confirmation is still needed, and LLM latency, Auto STT accuracy, full TTS continuity, and telephone playback remain open.

### 26 Oct 2026 local Qwen caller-state reminder

The mixed-script digit collector now normalizes a complete phone number into call state, but the local Qwen request previously received only the raw recognized utterance. Added a request-scoped system reminder containing only validated call-scoped caller name and complete 10–15 digit phone values. It is inserted immediately before the latest user turn, leaving the earlier prompt prefix unchanged for Ollama KV-cache reuse, and instructs the model not to ask again unless corrected or read the full number aloud. Strengthened the stable Qwen response guard to explicitly avoid echoing caller words and inventing service names.

Focused local tests for `test_contact_context.py` and `test_booking_tool_choice.py` passed: 46 passed, with one upstream Pipecat deprecation warning. The guard test initially exposed stale expected wording; the guard was aligned with its tested behavior and the suite then passed. The suite used dummy test-only database/Redis URLs and did not connect to production. After confirming zero production active calls, backed up server source under `/root/kodewaves-rollback/contact-context-20261026/`, copied only the two Python modules, and restarted only the API. Production helper replay confirmed that local Qwen gets the validated number reminder immediately before the latest user turn while the source conversation remains unchanged. API health is `ok`, container state is healthy with zero restarts, and active calls are zero. This is prompt-state evidence, not proof that a caller heard a correct follow-up; a new call is still needed. TTS playback gaps, STT auto-detection errors and end-to-end delay remain open.

### 26 Oct 2026 Anuvani-LID confidence calibration

Rechecked the deployed ONNX metadata against the Anuvani-LID paper. The model emits raw logits, while the published release applies temperature scaling `T=0.674` for calibrated confidence. The STT adapter previously softmaxed the raw logits directly before applying confidence gates, so its gate values did not follow the model's published calibration. Added the temperature scaling before log-softmax; this preserves top-1 labels but makes confidence-gated fallback/stability use the published calibration. Focused numeric check passed (`softmax([0,1]/0.674) = [0.18487, 0.81513]`), and Python syntax compilation passed.

With production active calls at zero, backed up source and tagged the old STT image as `kodewaves-indic-stt:rollback-lid-calibration-20261026`, rebuilt only the Indic STT image and recreated only `kodewaves_indic_stt`. New image digest: `sha256:fa0c05cbc99e84c1e4fc7461dd3297a5a5fd82911fcb5c54e0f2c87e07744868`. The service reports healthy, zero restarts, auto-detection enabled, and all 22 scheduled-language codes plus English installed. The production helper check returned the configured `T=0.674` probabilities. This corrects calibration only; it does not change top-1 language accuracy. A fresh caller test is still needed to verify Hindi/English/Hinglish routing, latency and TTS continuity. Source paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20263 .

At 11:12 UTC, rechecked the protected active-call endpoint and recent API/STT logs after the user offered another call. Active calls remained zero and there were no transcription or non-health API events in the previous 30 minutes; production UI/API/STT containers remained healthy. No live acceptance result is available yet.

### 26 Oct 2026 Workflow 22 historical trace re-audit (pre latest API restart)

Re-read the API container trace stream after the user offered a fresh test. The latest Workflow 22 run visible in the bounded two-hour log window is run 416 at 08:29–08:31 UTC; the current API container started at 10:10 UTC, so run 416 is historical and is not acceptance evidence for the post-restart language-routing fix. At audit time the protected active-call endpoint reported zero active calls and loop-lag p95/max of 2.07/2.28 ms. No newer Workflow 22 trace is present after that container start.

Run 416 provides concrete failure evidence: in auto mode, the IndicConformer language label changed among Marathi, Urdu, Hindi, Malayalam, Tamil and back to Marathi across short utterances. Most STT requests took 0.36–2.09 s; some exceeded 1.5 s. LLM first-output latency was 7.29 s for an incomplete stream, 6.31 s with a 1,263-token prompt (719 cached; 4,365 system-prompt characters; two tools), then 0.83–1.51 s on later requests as 1,259–1,514 prompt tokens were cached. On the latter requests, generation still took about 1.3–2.25 s after first output. This confirms a cold/uncached prompt-prefill penalty and misses the 700–800 ms full first-response target; the short-prompt candidate benchmark does not represent this load.

The audio transport itself did not show repeated packet starvation in most recorded turns: turns 9–11 had TTS first PCM in 268–465 ms, max PCM gaps of 224–648 ms, and zero gaps over 750 ms. However, turn 13 had 4,413 ms to first PCM and a 974 ms PCM gap, consistent with a slow/cold regional voice route; turn 9's first-speech frame was 7,302 ms after the observed output interval began despite a continuous downstream frame cadence. TTS is therefore inconsistent and the end-to-end wait remains high even when the final output transport has no gaps. This supports retaining the confidence-gated TTS route fix while investigating why the current IndicConformer detector has unstable language labels and why the first LLM prompt is partially uncached.

No code/configuration changed during this audit. The fresh live-call acceptance gate remains open; do not describe run 416 as a post-fix test.

### 26 Oct 2026 uncertain STT to TTS route correction and co-load probe

Before changing production, a short concurrent synthetic probe through the existing VPS services returned HTTP 200: Qwen first text in 475 ms and a complete 17-token response in 1,518 ms; Hindi TTS first PCM in 327 ms, 101,760 bytes (about 2.12 seconds of mono 24 kHz PCM) over 1,340 ms, with a maximum HTTP chunk gap of 271 ms. The Qwen prompt had only 49 tokens, 45 served from cache. This is a warm short-prompt provider co-load measurement, not a live workflow or audio-device acceptance, and it does not account for the 1,314-token prompt measured in Workflow 22 run 408.

Code review found that a low-confidence first IndicConformer label could still be exposed as the TTS language even when it was not accepted as stable. The gateway then used that guess to choose a regional speaker/model; run 416 had a Marathi-routed phrase whose first PCM took 4,413 ms. Also, `normalize_language_code('auto')` resolves to `en`, so the TTS resolver could turn the automatic setting into a false English hint. Updated the STT adapter to track a separate confidence-gated `latest_tts_language`, while retaining the best-effort transcript language for LLM routing; weak first guesses now leave script/preferred-voice routing in control, and low-confidence later fragments keep the prior stable TTS language. Automatic sentinels are detected before normalization, and the STT adapter treats both unset and explicit `auto` values as automatic mode. Added focused tests for weak/strong language switches and automatic TTS routing. Local focused suite passed: 52 tests, one upstream deprecation warning.

With production active-call telemetry at zero, saved rollback sources at `/root/kodewaves-rollback/tts-language-confidence-20261026/speaches_stt.py.pre` and `speaches_tts.py.pre`, copied only the two tested Python sources into the existing server checkout, verified remote syntax and matching SHA-256 hashes, and restarted only `kodewaves_api`. The API reports healthy, zero restarts, public health HTTP 200; protected active-call telemetry is zero. A 15-minute post-deployment log search still found no new STT/LLM/TTS/output trace, so caller-heard voice consistency and delay remain unverified. No model, organization setting, TTS service, other container, commit, or GitHub push changed.

Used the same synthetic 4,365-character workflow-sized system prompt and no customer data. `ollama show` reported 752.39M parameters, Q8_0, 1.0 GB model size.

Cold Hindi: 13.35 s total (3.37 s model load, 6.92 s prompt evaluation for 939 tokens, 3.03 s generation for 48 tokens); output had unnatural grammar and asked an irrelevant follow-up. Warm Hinglish/English: 7.13 s total (4.10 s prompt evaluation for 933 tokens, 3.01 s for 48 output tokens); answer was awkward and invented availability. Tool-confirmed booking: 13.37 s total (10.50 s prompt evaluation for 1,249 tokens, 2.73 s generation) and no structured tool call; response incorrectly said it could not book and re-asked for details already included. This candidate is slower at first response than the 2B Q4 production trace, fails call quality and tool acceptance, and is rejected. The candidate container was removed automatically after the trial; production Qwen, API, model selection, and settings remained unchanged.

### 26 Oct 2026 deployed STT/TTS inventory and synthetic auto-detection cross-check

The combined local TTS catalog reports 15 codes: `as,bn,brx,doi,en,hi,kn,mai,ml,mr,ne,pa,sa,ta,te`, matching the user's stated exclusions (Gujarati, Kashmiri, Konkani, Manipuri, Odia, Santali, Sindhi, Urdu). Hinglish is a Hindi/English mixed-text route, not a standalone language model.

Ran a synthetic end-to-end language-ID check: generated Hindi, Indian-English, Hinglish, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada and Malayalam utterances from the deployed combined TTS, then sent them through deployed IndicConformer in auto mode. Hindi and Tamil returned their expected labels in the first sample; English was labeled Hindi with confidence 0.426 and decoded into Devanagari phonetics; Hinglish was labeled Gujarati with confidence 0.347 and transcribed into Gujarati script; Telugu, Kannada and Malayalam were mislabeled in that run. A repeat English sample was labeled Hindi at 0.482 by the API; direct LID inspection of another synthesis of the same sentence ranked Telugu 0.757, Hindi 0.132, and English 0.016. A Hinglish repeat ranked Hindi 0.503, Gujarati 0.227. These are synthetic TTS samples, not caller speech, but they prove the current auto-LID can confidently misroute Indian-accent English and code-switching; a confidence threshold alone cannot fix these cases. Forced English Moonshine recognized the same synthetic English phrase mostly correctly (`Back, I can help you with your room booking.`), while forced Hindi produced the phonetic Devanagari transcript. This points to false auto-language labels/decoder selection as the cause of wrong-language replies and voice accent changes. No production config or code was changed by these probes.

### 26 Oct 2026 Indus CPU thread comparison

To investigate call 417's 5,805 ms second-phrase wall time, benchmarked the exact installed Inaya weights in a disposable server container with 2 CPU quota and shared read-only model weights. After a short warm request, the same long Hindi phrase took 1,258 ms total and 62 ms to first generated chunk with one PyTorch thread; two threads took 2,300 ms total and 92 ms to first chunk. Output audio durations differed slightly (5,280/4,960 ms), so this is a limited performance comparison, but it rejects increasing Indus thread count as an immediate speed fix on this host. The disposable container exited; live Indus config stayed at one thread. The earlier 5.8-second live phrase remains unexplained by model compute alone and may involve request queuing or concurrent serving. A new real call with provider queue timings is still needed to distinguish those causes.

### 26 Oct 2026 live Indus queue timing

Verified the current catalog only offers installed `qwen3:0.6b` and active `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M`; the old rollback tag is absent. Kept the small model for later quality/latency comparison because removing its disk files would not reduce the resident Qwen 2B RAM or solve call latency. Added content-free per-request timing to the Indus sidecar: input character count, wait on its synthesis lock, first PCM, synthesis total and chunk count. Production source backup is `/root/kodewaves-rollback/indus-queue-trace-20261026/app.py`; previous image is `kodewaves-indus-tts:rollback-queue-trace-20261026`. Rebuilt/recreated only Indus; both Indus and the combined TTS service returned healthy with zero restarts. A direct server probe returned HTTP 200, 126,720 PCM bytes, first HTTP audio in 207 ms, total 1,417 ms; the sidecar logged `queue_ms=0`, first internal PCM 84 ms and synthesis 1,390 ms for 33 characters. This confirms the trace works, but the 5.8-second live-call delay still requires a fresh WebRTC/telephone trace to attribute it to queueing versus synthesis. No log text, caller audio, tests or report was pushed.

Two concurrent synthetic 76-character Indus requests then confirmed the single-worker queue behavior: the first logged `queue_ms=0`, `synthesis_ms=2254`; the second logged `queue_ms=2253`, `synthesis_ms=2392`, `total_ms=4645`. The client received first audio at 169 ms and 2,355 ms respectively. A second in-flight phrase can therefore accrue seconds of latency even while the model itself synthesizes at a normal rate. This is a reproduction of provider queueing, not proof that call 417's long phrase had this cause; the next real call's new `queue_ms` field will distinguish it. No concurrency limit or model setting was changed from this test.

### 26 Oct 2026 safe two-request Hindi/English TTS serving

The installed Pocket TTS source explicitly says `generate_audio_stream` is **not thread-safe** on one model instance, so a fast shared-instance concurrency experiment was rejected. A disposable 2-CPU test using two separate loaded instances peaked at about 1.53 GiB RSS versus 1.22 GiB with one instance; both began generation in roughly 140 ms and completed in about 3.4 seconds. Two simultaneously generated Hindi clips both transcribed back to the complete intended sentence. A warm synthetic Qwen co-load comparison showed first text at 371 ms with two active TTS generations versus 156 ms alone; the initial cold-prompt measurement was not used as a comparable baseline. This tradeoff requires monitoring on real calls.

Implemented a bounded, configurable two-instance Indus pool with a dedicated one-thread executor per instance; interrupted requests retain their model slot until generator cleanup finishes. The combined VITS gateway previously serialized all TTS through one lock and worker, so it now uses a separate two-request lane only when every segment routes to the bilingual Indus voice. Regional synthesis remains on its existing single-worker lane. The shared compose defaults remain one worker/one CPU for other installations; this VPS sets `INDUS_WORKERS=2` and `INDUS_CPU_LIMIT=2.0` in its private `.env`, and the combined gateway uses the same count. The production Indus container remains within its 2 GiB memory limit; the disposable HTTP service was around 1.2 GiB at idle and after its two-request probe.

Rollback source/compose/env and image tags are at `/root/kodewaves-rollback/indus-two-worker-20261026/`, `/root/kodewaves-rollback/combined-two-worker-20261026/`, `kodewaves-indus-tts:rollback-two-worker-20261026`, and `kodewaves-indic-vits-tts:rollback-two-worker-20261026`. Production API, combined TTS and Indus are healthy. Two simultaneous 76-character requests through the **production combined gateway** both returned HTTP 200 and started client PCM in 362 and 270 ms, completed in 3,496 and 3,483 ms, and logged zero Indus queue wait. Before this change the second direct Indus request started audio at 2,355 ms and completed at 4,653 ms. Those are synthetic concurrent service requests; caller-heard WebRTC/telephone continuity, one-call phrase tail latency, regional-language interaction and the 700–800 ms end-to-end goal remain unverified. Disposable experiment images, containers and directories were removed; rollback copies remain. No tests, diagnostics or reports were pushed.

Follow-up code trace of the Pipecat TTS wrapper confirms that its `_process_text_frame` awaits `_push_tts_frames`, which awaits `tts_process_generator` until each phrase's HTTP generator completes. Thus this two-worker deployment removes cross-call/overlapping-turn provider queueing but does not make consecutive phrases from one Pipecat TTS instance synthesize in parallel. Its effect on the user's single-call stop/play complaint must be measured rather than inferred from the two-request service probe. The next core task is to compare new `local_tts_phrase_trace` and Indus `queue_ms` on a real turn and address phrase sequencing if the gap remains.

A post-deploy simultaneous mixed Hindi/English request and Kannada regional request through the combined production endpoint both returned HTTP 200, with 127,200 and 132,608 PCM bytes in 818 and 1,082 ms respectively. This confirms the separate bilingual and regional lanes can serve together; it does not assess naturalness or caller-heard timing.

Rechecked run 417's stage and phrase logs before making another TTS change. ### 26 Oct 2026 smaller Qwen3.5 model rejection and unused-model cleanup

Read-only inspection of Workflow 22's current pinned definition found a 1,982-character start-node prompt; the composed prompt also includes 914 characters of common grounding rules, 187 characters of no-action guidance, and a 418-character voice-persona rule, before runtime routing/clock/contact additions. The system instruction measured 4,365 characters in the actual LLM trace. This explains why a small CPU model must prefill a much larger prompt than the short standalone examples, but does not itself justify deleting user-authored workflow instructions.

Checked the [official Ollama Qwen3.5 model listing](https://ollama.com/library/qwen3.5), which offers a 0.8B variant. With zero active calls and 66 GB available disk, downloaded it to the VPS for a temporary comparison without changing the selected organization model. On three synthetic booking/language cases through Ollama with reasoning disabled, the 0.8B model took 11,653 ms for an awkward Hindi first question, 3,651 ms for an English reply that asked for the date even though the caller said "tomorrow", and 16,998 ms for a Hindi follow-up that repeated the known phone and distorted the date question. These are narrow quality checks, but they contradict the assumption that the smaller variant solves speed while retaining required booking behavior. ### 26 Oct 2026 English auto-detection rescue

Production inventory exposes 15 paired STT/TTS languages: Assamese, Bengali, Bodo, Dogri, English, Hindi, Kannada, Maithili, Malayalam, Marathi, Nepali, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Tamil and Telugu. Direct synthesized phrase probes returned nonempty PCM for all 15 and auto STT selected the expected language on each regional preview. This is model-generated audio, not human speech acceptance. Current regional TTS returned first PCM only after synthesis (about 670–1540 ms in these previews); bilingual Indus began earlier (about 105–312 ms). No new WebRTC or telephone call occurred after the earlier TTS changes.

Reproduced an English routing fault: Anuvani LID labeled several clearly spoken Indian-English samples as Hindi or Telugu, even when the installed English Moonshine decoder transcribed them correctly. Evaluated a small Vakgyata language detector on the VPS, rejected it for misclassifying English/Hindi and multiple regional previews, and removed its temporary probe files. A Whisper tiny int8 language identifier (~99 MB total weights) identified four of five tested medium English clips as English, but missed “Hello, I need help with my order” and short “Yes”/“Thank you”; none of 11 tested regional previews was falsely labeled English. It is **not** a full multilingual replacement.

Added an optional, narrow Whisper English rescue to the STT service, with weights in the persistent model volume. It runs only when Anuvani chose another language and audio is at least one second; only a Whisper `en` decision overrides Anuvani. All other regional decisions remain with Anuvani. The Pipecat STT wrapper now accepts this route for its stable reply/TTS language. On the live endpoint, two Indian-English samples returned English transcripts through `whisper_english_rescue` in 357 and 315 ms; Hindi stayed Hindi in 541 ms and Tamil stayed Tamil in 903 ms. This validates those service calls only. Short English and code-switching inside an utterance remain unresolved; the secondary identifier adds CPU work, and real caller audio is needed to measure overall turn latency and false positives.

Before deployment active calls were zero. Rollback source and prior STT image are at `/root/kodewaves-rollback/whisper-english-rescue-20261026/` and `kodewaves-indic-stt:rollback-whisper-rescue-20261026`. Only STT was rebuilt/recreated and the API restarted; API, STT and combined TTS were healthy with zero restart counts afterward. No tests, diagnostic scripts or report were pushed to GitHub.

### 27 Oct 2026 recorded phone dictation fault and narrow repair

The actual Workflow 22 caller recording from run 438 showed a complete spoken phone phrase. Replaying its 38.14–44.26 second span through the production auto STT returned a full Hindi-script 10-digit phrase in 841 ms, but the live call had split the same dictation into short transcripts. Running the production Silero VAD over this recording produced three voice spans at the previous 200 ms stop setting and one span at a 550 ms stop setting. The current Workflow 22 definition does not override `vad_stop_secs`. The local segmented STT default was changed to 550 ms on the VPS and the API restarted; other streaming STT remains at 200 ms. This adds up to 350 ms of endpointing for local STT in exchange for fewer clipped utterances. No fresh live call has yet confirmed the effect.

The complete replay transcript also exposed a separate parser defect: it rendered “फ़ोन”, “फ़ाइव”, “ऐट”, and “फ़ोर”, which the phone collector rejected. The collector now normalizes Devanagari nukta only for parsing and recognizes “ऐट” as eight. The exact recognized phrase now returns 10 accepted digits and no repeat prompt in a direct local code check. Server source was patched with four exact replacements, syntax checked, and the API restart was requested. The final server-side combined replay could not finish because SSH and both public site endpoints began timing out. Server availability, fresh call behavior, and latency remain unverified; investigate the VPS outage before any further deployment. No tests or report were pushed.

Follow-up refinement: applying 550 ms globally would add 350 ms to every local-STT reply. The worktree now keeps the 200 ms default and changes to 550 ms only while call state says the agent is collecting a phone number; it changes back after the slot is complete. The Silero class smoke check confirmed both thresholds switch while idle. An exact replay-transcript parser regression and existing Hindi/Tamil/Gujarati digit tests passed (3 selected tests); syntax compilation passed. At that point the contact-context suite had two room-booking expectations that asked the appointment-only helper to handle a room reservation; those failures were subsequently resolved by correcting the test cases and adding a separate guard regression, recorded below. No booking guard was weakened.

Important deployment gap: the current VPS became unreachable before this refinement could be deployed. The VPS has the earlier 550 ms global VAD setting and the first server parser patch (nukta removal without NFC recomposition); the refined worktree restores NFC for regional-script tokens. Recheck VPS reachability, restore the refined VAD/parser files without overwriting server-specific edits, restart the API, and verify the saved phrase and a fresh call before claiming the fix is live.

### 27 Oct 2026 full-scheduled-language source audit

Compared the persistent 22-language goal against the active local serving code. `indic_stt/app.py` allows auto STT for 14 scheduled Indian languages plus English, despite Anuvani LID having more class labels. `indic_vits/app.py` has the same scheduled-language coverage through Rasa plus Hindi voices, with a separate Indian-English voice. **Gujarati, Kashmiri, Konkani, Manipuri, Odia, Santali, Sindhi and Urdu are not end-to-end local STT/TTS capabilities in this branch.** Auto-detecting a detector class without a matching decoder and speakable TTS voice would produce a false capability claim; leave these routes disabled until the complete path is installed and tested.

Read the official candidate sources. AI4Bharat Indic-TTS lists 15 languages including Gujarati, Manipuri and Odia but does not cover all remaining gaps, and no target-CPU/streaming benchmark is available. Indic-Speak advertises 22 Indian languages plus English, but its card lists 13 as production and 10 as preview, specifies a 3.78B BF16 speech LM plus a Vocos decoder, and its license restricts robocalls/auto-dialers and requires written approval for third-party hosted API use absent an exception. Because Kodewaves includes campaigns/telephony and targets a four-core CPU server, this candidate is **not cleared for production** without license/use-case review and measured CPU, memory, quality, and latency results. It was not downloaded, terms were not accepted, and no service/configuration was changed.

Also screened the current gated **Quipus 0.6B Speech v2** card. ### 27 Oct 2026 attached latency-audit reconciliation

Reviewed the attached `Self-Hosted STT, TTS and LLM Latency Audit & 1.5-Second Plan`. Its broad direction is useful: define latency from speech end to actual audible playback; correlate VAD, STT, LLM, TTS, queue and transport stages; compare full workflow prompts and cache state; profile combined CPU/memory at concurrency; and require quality plus browser/telephone acceptance before claiming a latency target. These become the working measurement and acceptance criteria for this voice track.

The attachment identifies itself as a read-only scan of the `stabilize` checkout. Its source inspection does not match this active `codex/production-integration` checkout in at least two material places, so those findings must not be copied as current defects:

- **Auto STT:** the attachment says local auto detection is disabled. Current `api/local_models/indic_stt/app.py` has Anuvani LID and auto routing, with `AUTO_DETECT_LANGUAGES` set to 15 installed voice-supported codes (`as`, `bn`, `brx`, `doi`, `en`, `hi`, `kn`, `mai`, `ml`, `mr`, `ne`, `pa`, `sa`, `ta`, `te`). The production Model Configuration UI was separately observed showing multilingual auto detection and 15 installed languages. This confirms the selector/routing capability exists; per-language transcript accuracy and uninterrupted multilingual conversation are still unaccepted.
- **TTS text release:** the attachment says the live TTS aggregator only has a 20-character first chunk and can wait until final punctuation. Current `api/services/pipecat/speaches_tts.py` configures first-clause, first-phrase and later streaming-phrase thresholds (36/36/64 characters, with a 12-character first chunk); `LocalSpeechAggregator` implements word-boundary partial release. `api/tests/test_natural_local_tts_phrases.py` passed its focused test in this worktree. That verifies the local aggregation path, not that production is running this exact source or that live playback has no gaps.

The recommendations that remain open are **measurement and end-to-end verification**, not repeating those two source changes: obtain a fresh correlated browser and telephone trace with real speech-end and first non-silent playout markers; measure LLM prompt prefill/cache and first speakable phrase on the actual workflow; measure TTS phrase queue/gaps through client/carrier playback; and profile combined service CPU/memory and queueing at supported concurrency. Historical slow Qwen and telephone evidence remains valid only for its recorded source/configuration and is not a post-change baseline.

**Acceptance remains open.** There is no verified post-change caller-heard run in this evidence set, no p50/p95 result for the 1.5-second target, and no basis to claim all 15 auto-routed languages are accurate or that telephony is fixed. The VPS is currently unreachable from this workspace, so the refined phone-aware VAD and parser changes in this branch are not verified as deployed. Preserve the attached audit as a reference; use this reconciliation as the branch-specific direction. No production files, model inventory, or organization settings were changed by reviewing the audit, and no audit/report/diagnostic was pushed.

### 27 Oct 2026 ambiguous spoken phone digit correction

The live parser treated the ASR tokens `on`, `run`, `won` and `wan` as the digit 1 and could save a complete number without verifying that guess. A pre-fix production-source test reproduced this for the user's observed `nine-four on` pattern. Added a focused guard while collecting phone numbers: if a mostly numeric utterance includes one of these ambiguous tokens, clear its partial accumulation and request the full number again; do not store a guessed number. Explicit unrelated requests retain normal routing, and a clear repeat is accepted. This avoids a wrong saved contact but does not improve acoustic recognition and may require an extra user turn.

### 27 Oct 2026 sequential Hindi TTS intermittent stall

On the quiet VPS, checked zero active calls before each request and streamed three synthetic Hindi phrases directly through the production combined TTS gateway. ### 27 Oct 2026 Indus decoder batch bound — production

Traced the installed pocket_tts decoder: its model default `max_decoder_frames_per_call=0` means an unlimited drain of queued latent frames into one decode. In a deterministic preloaded-queue check, 15 pending frames produced one 15-frame decode and one output chunk with the default; a bound of one produced 15 single-frame decodes and 15 output chunks. This mechanism can delay initial output when a backlog forms and is consistent with (but does not prove the cause of) the preceding 10.2/6.1-second first-PCM observations with two large chunks.

Built and started a disposable server-only candidate with one frame per decode and the same model files. A warm direct comparison under equal 2-CPU limits measured first PCM 89–104 ms for candidate versus 99–177 ms current across three phrases; candidate completion was 1.53/1.75/2.10 s versus 0.97/1.61/2.12 s, showing possible overhead on a short sample. At two simultaneous direct candidate requests, first PCM was 137/177 ms and total 3.35/3.55 s for 4.72/5.20 s audio; current was 281/143 ms and 3.93/3.85 s for 4.96/5.12 s audio. These are small synthetic samples, not a quality, p95 or real-call acceptance test. The candidate container and its temporary files were removed.

Changed Indus startup to use `INDUS_DECODER_FRAMES`, bounded to 1–8, default 1. Rebuilt a small derived image from the installed image, preserved original source and image tag under /root/kodewaves-rollbacks/tts-decoder-20261027-180416 (`kodewaves-indus-tts:rollback-decoder-180416`), then recreated only Indus after zero-active-call checks. Both workers completed startup warmup. Three post-deployment production-gateway synthetic Hindi requests yielded nonempty PCM with first client bytes 240/186/142 ms and max arrival gaps 110/90/110 ms. Two concurrent gateway requests yielded first bytes 296/207 ms, total 4.05/4.38 s for 4.66/5.71 s audio, and maximum individual arrival gaps 338/146 ms. API and Indus reported healthy; Indus restart count was zero. These validate a bounded production path, not caller-heard continuity, speech naturalness, regional TTS, real-user latency or the 700–800 ms target. The original severe stall's full cause remains unproven; continue observing and use the preserved rollback if performance or quality regresses. No tests, diagnostics or reports were pushed.

## 2026-10-28 TTS phrase-gap evidence check

- The deployed `SpeachesLiveTTSService` starts streaming the first phrase after a 200 ms PCM buffer and processes local speech requests sequentially. A 200 ms cushion cannot by itself cover a later synthesis gap longer than 200 ms; this is a plausible source of audible stop/start, not yet a proven live-call cause.
- Queried the production API container logs for `local_tts_phrase_trace` over the last seven days. No matching traces were present. Thus there is no post-deployment phrase timing evidence to confirm a gap, and it would be misleading to claim a continuity fix from code inspection alone.
- Next required evidence: a fresh WebRTC/telephone call after the current tracing deployment, with per-phrase first PCM and wall times plus transport playback gaps, before changing buffer size or synthesis scheduling.

## 2026-10-28 correction to direct Indus timing

- Repeated the same two short Hindi phrases with 1,024-byte streaming reads. First PCM arrived in 237 ms and 194 ms; full generation took 958 ms and 946 ms for 2.07 s and 2.26 s of audio. The earlier 7.1/5.7 s figures from 9,600-byte reads were transient/outlier observations, not a stable first-audio baseline. Do not use them as proof that Indus always takes several seconds.
- In this idle direct-service check, synthesis completed faster than playback length. Sequential requests and a 200 ms first buffer therefore do not by themselves prove the reported live stop/start. Need a correlated live call showing TTS frame timing and downstream playback/cancellation to locate the audible gap.

## 2026-10-28 WebRTC underrun instrumentation status

- Local instrumentation counts 10 ms auto-silence frames from `RawAudioTrack` while the downstream output transport marks bot speech active, then logs one aggregate at the stopped-speaking marker. It records no transcript or audio bytes.
- `py -3.13 -m py_compile pipecat/src/pipecat/transports/smallwebrtc/transport.py` passed. No focused SmallWebRTC tests exist in this repository, and pytest is not installed in the Windows interpreter. The change remains local and has not been deployed.
- Production WebRTC queue underruns remain unmeasured; use a real call after this instrumentation is run through the deployment's normal build and verification path.

- Follow-up instrumentation review: the marker lifecycle originates in BaseOutputTransport when it processes actual audio, and its downstream `BotStartedSpeakingFrame` is queued through the output sender. The local counter is scoped to that output track and starts/stops on those downstream markers; syntax compilation passed after the review.

- Corrected telemetry timing: the downstream speaking marker is queued after the first audio and cannot be used to reset counters without losing the initial queue gap. The local track now starts/reset counters when its first audio bytes are enqueued, and records speech interval duration with the underrun summary. Syntax compile passes. Still local only; a live call is required before deploying.

- Counter simulation attempt timed out because aiortc track initialization uses real-time scheduling even for an isolated `recv()` call. The module import and bytecode compile succeeded; no deterministic behavioral test completed. Instrumentation remains local and unverified for deployment.

- Deterministic counter branch check passed: three simulated empty 10 ms queue slots incremented total and maximum consecutive underruns; adding and consuming queued audio reset the current streak. Module import/bytecode compile and `git diff --check` passed. This validates telemetry arithmetic only, not aiortc/browser playback or real-call behavior.

- Counter lifecycle corrected again: the output sender’s `BotStoppedSpeakingFrame` reflects queue timeout and can fire between sequential TTS phrases; it would prematurely terminate accounting. Aggregate telemetry now closes on queued `TTSStoppedFrame`, after the sender flushes trailing PCM. Local compile and diff whitespace checks pass. No production deployment or live call has occurred.

- Final telemetry lifecycle now marks the end at `TTSStoppedFrame` but keeps the speech interval active until RawAudioTrack's queued 10 ms chunks drain. If audio resumes before drain, the pending end is canceled, so phrase boundaries do not prematurely close the measurement. Syntax and whitespace checks pass. Real WebRTC behavior still needs one call.

- Removed the temporary drain timer after review identified a cancellation race. Metrics now close synchronously when the track consumes the final queued 10 ms chunk after the end marker; new audio before drain cancels the pending end. Local compile and whitespace checks pass.

- Added focused `TestRawAudioTrackUnderrunMetrics` to the existing SmallWebRTC transport test module. It verifies real `add_audio_bytes` queue setup, an ongoing sample across phrase audio, preservation of maximum consecutive silence count, and immediate close when `TTSStoppedFrame` arrives after the queue has drained. Focused pytest passed (1/1); `git diff --check` passed. This validates local queue accounting only; no live browser or server deployment has been done.

- Expanded local queue telemetry tests to cover both end-marker orderings: after queue drain (immediate summary) and before drain (remain active until final queued chunks are consumed). Focused pytest passed 2/2; both files compile; `git diff --check` passed. Still no live WebRTC browser sample or production deployment.

### 2026-10-30 production Indic STT residency and cold-load gate

- Read-only production snapshot supplied by the platform integration task: API image `kodewaves-api:contact-capture-10a1452ef`; STT image `kodewaves-indic-stt:phone-rescue-cleaner-20261029`; UI image `kodewaves-ui:candidate-theme-polish`. Both `kodewaves_indic_vits_tts` and `kodewaves_indus_tts` were running. Ollama had `qwen3.5:2b-q4_K_M` resident at 1.6 GB, using CPU with context 4096.
- The live Indic STT `/health` response, queried from the API container, advertised installed and auto-detect support for `as,bn,brx,doi,en,hi,kn,mai,ml,mr,ne,pa,sa,ta,te` (14 scheduled Indian languages plus English), while only `hi,en` were resident/warm. Installed/advertised coverage therefore does not imply a decoder is resident or has completed its first decode.
- The same idle snapshot reported API memory 1.014/2 GiB, STT 862 MiB/2 GiB, Ollama 2.27/4 GiB; host memory 9 GiB/15 GiB used, 5.7 GiB available, no swap. This is spare-capacity evidence at idle, not a peak or concurrent-call budget; any additional resident decoders must be measured under load before changing the cap.
- Likely cold-path mechanism in the current candidate implementation: `api/local_models/indic_stt/app.py` initializes the `resident_languages` set with startup prewarm voices (default Hindi and English); other installed languages are instantiated lazily by `_load()` during `decode()`. The endpoint's `decode_ms` timer begins before `decode()`, so it includes lazy recognizer construction as well as inference. `_load()` applies an LRU bounded by `INDIC_STT_RESIDENT_MODELS`; an evicted regional decoder can incur construction cost again when selected later. The service's health manifest reports installed languages separately from `_loaded`, matching the live `installed_languages` versus `resident_languages` discrepancy. Confirm the deployed image's code/config before assuming it has these exact mechanics.
- Existing controlled synthetic evidence in the prior candidate recorded first Bengali decode around 17.8 s and first Tamil decode around 10.0 s, dropping after warmup to about 116 ms and 113 ms explicit-language decode respectively; the associated traces attributed the large cold costs to decoder work, while queue time was 0–1 ms. That is evidence that cold inference/loading is a real candidate cause, but the latest live snapshot alone does not measure cold latency, and synthetic audio does not prove caller quality or production p50/p95.
- Before replacing or promoting the recognizer, capture one correlated same-audio cold/warm pair per representative regional language. Record image digest, effective resident/prewarm environment, pre/post `/health`, `queue_ms`, `lid_ms`, `decode_ms`, wall time, and STT container memory. If the first-call delay remains inside `decode_ms` after queue and LID are low, add a separate recognizer-construction timer in an isolated candidate to distinguish graph load from first-inference setup; do not infer this from the aggregate alone. Repeat across language switches to detect LRU re-eviction and compare memory/latency under concurrency 1 and 2. Retain the current model until this diagnosis and language-quality checks are complete.
- This section records measurements supplied by the integration task and a source-level diagnosis; no production config, image, model, or running container was changed by this work.

### 2026-10-30 Auto selector investigation and branch safety

- UI source path: `GET /catalog/available` determines `local_stt_models`; the API copies `supports_auto_detect` from the selected installed STT sidecar model manifest. The UI exposes `multi` only when the selected catalog model has boolean `supports_auto_detect: true` and at least one installed supported language. The saved STT model is matched by exact ID, falling back to the first installed local STT model.
- The live sidecar manifest, endpoint environment, and deployed API selector implementation agree with this contract. The exact authenticated catalog JSON received by the logged-in browser remains the decisive missing evidence: it must include `has_local_ai_access`, `local_engine_enabled`, `local_stt_models[].value`, `supports_auto_detect`, and the saved `stt_model`. Desktop browser inspection is disabled in this Work profile, so I did not access that response or alter the user's settings.

### Current measured live voice issues

- Recent numeric LLM traces show first output around 0.74–7.17 seconds and complete generation around 2.28–9.59 seconds. Prompt sizes are roughly 844–2,427 tokens with partial prefix-cache reuse. This is the largest measured delay; the requested 700–800 ms end-to-end target is not met.
- Recent STT traces include empty recognition rows and a multi-second Marathi outlier; automatic detection and number/date capture are not qualified across the advertised language set.
- TTS produced PCM in sampled traces, but first PCM varied up to about 1.9 seconds and service synthesis up to about 3.9 seconds. Transport frame-arrival gaps were below 750 ms in the sampled output traces, while internal low-energy silence reached about 30 seconds. These measurements are not correlated to one caller turn, so do not attribute the whole gap to transport or call this fixed.
- Current production still runs API/UI/STT/TTS/Ollama images from the `2511131b` release checkout. Health is reported green, but that does not prove browser/telephone audio acceptance. Live image tag and source must be reported separately because tags are not reliable source identifiers.

### End-to-end acceptance board

- [ ] Self-hosted CPU STT/TTS/LLM: validate model inventory and warm/cold resource use on the target VPS and local PC without disrupting either environment; preserve customer data and rollback assets.
- [ ] Language coverage: validate all 22 scheduled Indian languages individually, Indian-accented English and Hindi/English code-switching for STT accuracy, reply language, TTS availability, voice consistency and grammar. Publish only capabilities that pass; capture any exclusions and fallbacks here.
- [ ] Conversation: resolve long first-token latency, empty replies, repeated prompts, conversation-memory loss, hallucinated dates/times and phone-number recognition/readback.
- [ ] Speech: verify natural speech, stable voice/gender across language changes, first non-silent audio, complete TTS playback, phrase gaps, overlap, barge-in cancellation and no stale audio.
- [ ] WebRTC: run a fresh browser microphone call after deployment; correlate speech end, STT final, LLM first useful text, TTS first non-silent PCM and output playback for each turn.
- [ ] Telephony: run an authorized telephone call; verify greeting/announcement timing, language and voice stability, follow-ups, name/number/date capture, no overlap/cutoff and complete caller-heard playback.
- [ ] Report p50/p95 from representative warm and cold turns at concurrency 1 and 2; no end-to-end latency target is accepted from isolated provider timings.
- [ ] User/admin pages: verify settings; providers/model/voice synchronization; agent create/edit; campaigns; telephony; tools; files and recordings; CRM; appointments; forms; widgets; templates; runs; billing; reports; owner/superadmin controls; public pages and client installation.
- [ ] Client deployment/security: test fresh install, upgrade, rollback, license and owner-key rotation, domain/IP whitelist, alert delivery, tenant isolation, local data ownership, branding controls and removal of setup scripts after install.
- [ ] Confirm text chat, integrations, database migrations, recordings/storage retention, billing and access control remain intact after release.

All unchecked items remain open. Update this ledger only with dated evidence, exact refs/images, test scope and remaining limitations. Do not claim full readiness from source merge, image build, health checks or partial call tests.
